LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAR NELLIE,

There are a number of charming frocks and coats for children and young girls about. Dresse3 of sky blue velveteen, with vests of various coleueed silks and bands, are noticeable on the young folks. I saw one with revers of blue velvet, embroidered in white and blue silk, and similar embroidery ou the alcoves at the wrists. A coat a friend of hlir.e was buying for her little sister was comp.:sei of white wool repp, trimmed with kilted trills of white silk on the cuffs and round the cape. Above the kiltings there were narrow bands of beaver fur. She was also purchasing a quaint little bonnet of hlack velvet, trimmed and lined with soft white •v. ngal satin. lam of the opinien, however, that tt of another shape would have looked equally

I cannot say that anything particularly striking has occurred in regard to chapeaux lately. Everyone has them trimmed according to their own fancy. I think a pretty hat could bc ramie of green velvet, for I have one of tl,.kt clo.r. tin each side is a bird's head and a buret: a greaa and brown feathers, arranged rat'aer flat at the I.:les. A friend of mine has one of biovin f-it cloth, with - a twist of bright blue velvet itt--oat the c..t.rrn. The trimming is of blue tulle, a 0 1 ght-blite, and brown wings, and it is v.:ranv-.4 ba sid,. Felt cloth in castor forms anothtz Lat ith a rcflla brim turned up with a brown sel:et painted feather. These imitation feathers, pAnted on both gauze and velvet, will be much worn, to judge by the number appearing on the early models.. Ropes of ptrple velvet, woven into a lattice-work, make a round chapeau that is trimmed only by a long, painted feather of white gauze.

A large number of splendid fur jackets are now being shown in many establishments, but by reason of the comparative mildness of the weather they have not been universally adopted. A coat I saw yesterday was a combination of fur in grey lamb with brown fox, a small pelerine being formed with a yoke of lamb, the shoulders outlined with the whole fox, the long tail falling to the knees. The beat of the dark fur coats, the sealskin, and the broad-tail ttre faced with ermine—ermine with- out the little black tails—and sometimes they are bordered again with a narrow strip of dark fur. The bolero coat obtains exclusively for the furs, to the waist at the back, and pointing downwards in the front. Broad-tail is perhaps the most becom- ing, for lb euitg the feminine figure, if not the feminine face.

As very wide toques make some faoes look charming, while they render others much too small by force of contrast so the present mode of doing the hair has much the same effect. By affording a large frame to the face, it reduces its apparent size. But when this tends to insignificance, it leaves the "mignonne" behind. The very small-featured should avoid the very liberal coiffure end the hat to match. Ladies with Roman noses may wear their hair wide disprehrl, and a liberal envelopment of hat. Now that Lroheads have reappeared after an eclipse of several years, a very large face has its only chanze of looking well by adopting a coiffure of adequate size. Perched on this, a small toque looks ridiculous. A very high authority on dress and beauty says that the size of the headgear should be regulated by that of the face. A eleven and experienced English woman declares that the character cf the headdress and coiffure should, first of all, depend on the siie and form of the shoulders, and the length or otherwise of the neck. The stature has also to be taken into account.

A hat of white glace, with black stitchings upon it, with a border of black tulle, a flame-coloured rosette, and a pair of black and white quills in the !rant is an odd fancy, but it obtains our patronage; indeed, absorbs it, together with the much twisted plateau and soft hat shapes made entirely of plaitings of velvet and tulle in the tiniest, nar- rowest straps.

A little boy's coat I saw recently was built on the lines of the so-much-liked highway- man's model, and with it there was a tricorne hat, composed of cloth to match the coat or of felt the same colour. Suitable hues for this design are castor, 'gazelle brown, putty, blue, scarlet, or locust green. Your loving JENNIIL

REVENGE OF VALERIE:

A ROMANCE OF BRITISH COLUMBIA.

BY HUME NISBET,

Author of "A Sweet Sinner," "Bail Up," "The Rebel Chief," "Kings of the Sea," 'Hunting for Gold," "The Swarupers,"

" Yes, governor, lii 0,, there." Walter Gordon glanced keenly round him as he lint there. There was a safe in this room, butt , was standing open, as if inviting inspection. ."No," he thought, "he doesn't keep any private papers here. They must be at his own house." ilHe rose and shook hands with his old employer and went out to meet his pal Solomon and concoct A plans. At ten o'clock, as Stephen Belwood was lingering 'and obliterating himself as much as possible in the ,drawing-room. a card was brought to him with Al" Mr. George Walters" on it. . "Shew the gentleman into my study, please," he maid to the domestic. "I will see him there."

;ti A few moments afterwards he glided out from ! that fashionable throng and sought his visitor. f;Walter Gordon had been there first, and looking round formed his own conclusions.

1' Stephen Belwood, after getting his visitor served with brandy and soda and cigars—he did not smoke iihimself, at least, not tobacco, neither did he drink spirits—started business. 11 They argued matters out for an hour, -before ' Walter Gordon said he was satisfied. He would I take a cheque for two thousand pounds, and a few i,payable directorships. - Then Stephen Belwood opened his private safe and got out his cheque-book, well pleased that he had managed affairs so well. He left Walter Gordon at the fireplace, smok- ing one of his finest cigars, while he turned his back upon him to fill up the cheque. He had just signed his name and was about to blot it, when suddenly a handkerchief steeped .with chloroform was pressed upon his mouth and nostrils, that in Ihis weakling condition he could not ward it off. A gasp or two, and Stephen Belwood dropped like a dead man, inider the grip of his powerful assailant. Walter Gordon held his victim with that saturated handkerchief over his mouth and nostrils for fully five minutes. Then, satisfied that he was past resistance, he carried him .over to the couch and laid him down there.

He next locked the door and began his search in the open safe. Stephen Belwood had opened it to Iget his cheque-book. At last he had secured the papers he wanted. He read them to see if all were right, and, satisfied, he crammed them into his inner coat pocket. . .

Then, with one last look at his unconscious victim on the couch, he opened the door, and walked quietly. down the staircase to the hall. . . .

I - The- hallkeeper let him out obsequiously, helping him on with his hat and coat. Then, quite satis- fied, he went out of the front door.

Two yards from the door, in the street, he found himself gripped and arrested for a little bit of private swindling which he had done before he had left England for Australia. Dastardly fate. It was only for three pounds five; and he had in his pocket a cheque for two thousand pounds, and documents enough to secure him a life of affluence. Walter cursed his luck as he resigned himself to his fate. Solomon Davies, who had been hanging about for his admired friend, skedaddled when he saw the arrest. Ho lay perdu for the next day until he received a message from his imprisoned friend through his lawyer. It ran thus: "See the Duchess and warn her. All up. Get what you can out of her to help mo, and vamouse." Solomon Davies took the hint and the first boat after this from Calais, and hied him straight south to !Italy, leaving Madame Davies to follow at her leisure.

===== 0002601_18991027_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XXXII.

STEPHEN BELWOOD AT HOME.

Although Stephen Belwood fixed the large amount of fifty thousand pounds as the price of her revenge to the Duchess, not a twentieth part of it left his own hands.

He satisfied the scruples of Mr. Algernon Churchill Champion by promoting him to the board of several new and sound companies, thereby increasing his income by some inure hundreds pet annum. He likewise gave him some sage hints ,14 to good investments, and thus secured the gratitud and silence of that gentleman. To the amiable spouse of this virtuous directe, he presented five hundred pounds worth of fresh jewellery. He did not think it necessary to give more, as for their own sakes they would keep quiet over that bit of perjury and baseness. Walter Gordon was the worst customer he had to deal with, this gentleman being in a number of his other secrets, and about as wily as he was himself.

He had to part with three thousand pounds before Walter even pretended to be satisfied. They had a bitter contest over this deal in the private office of the money-lending establishment, and said hard things to one another. Finally, however, Walter consented to take that sum as a compensa- tion for haring to leave his beloved London. It was necessary to get him out of the way as well as Mr. and Mrs. Solomon Davies, in case inquiries were afterwards instituted. The manager, Walter Gordon, felt it also wise that he should obliterate himself for awhile, since his name had bev mixed up with that of Hope Eastlake. Outs raged husbands are ugly customers for smooth- faced Lotharios to be interviewed by, and personal courage was not one of this young man's shining qualities. Therefore he reschristcned himself, as did his subordinate Solomon Davies. Mr. Stephen Bel- wood paid their passage out to Melbourne, and gave them employment there in a Mount of Pity branch establishment, which he commissioned his late manager to open in that gay city of the South. Walter Gordon took out his passage as George Walters, and his companions as Mr. and Mrs. Saul Denvers. One thousand pounds was their perquisite for helping to ruin Hope. The pawnbroker assistant who had identified Hope as the pledger of the jewellery, he bestowed twenty pounds upon, and gave him a rise in his profession with an increase of salary. Possibly he may have considered himself justly entitled to the bulk of those wages of sin, seeing that he was the master sinner. At any rate, he stuck to it as his legitimate rights. It was a large amount, certainly, but not too large for his com- mission, he thought, considering the risks he ran, if ever this plot was discovered. Having settled these matters to his own satisfac- tion, if not entirely .to that of his tools, he pro- ceeded to look after more regular business. Since his business relations with the Duchess Valerie good luck in his City speculations seemed to have turned entirely in his favour. Formerly he had had reverses as all speculators have at times, and met them with Stock Exchange philosophy. But now, and for the past year Pluto had adopted bins and imparted to him the gift of King Midas • whatever he touched changed into gold for him and his followers. For a year past he had not failed in a single.forecast. year schemes he took up or patronised were successes. This meant, of course, that he was accumulating wealth so rapidly that already it was beginning to lose Much of the pleasure it once had. He did not grow any more generous or philanthropic, how- ever. Ha was becoming, if anything, more penurious and mean about trifles. Of late, money-lenders had been having uncom- fortable times in the courts, and nasty exposures in the papers. As yet his business had escaped this inquisitorial handling, but he began to tremble lest his hour might copse. Stephen Belwood, HO City Colossus began to get 'a distaste for those' pettifogging and sordia. businesses' which his prudent father had left to him. For the sake of his name in the City and that fashionable dame who looked after his private mansion, he decided to get rid of those businesses as speedily as possible. Once decided the matter was easily done. He flung them upon the market and made them into companies. Shares were issued, and the capital speedily secured, and the price paid into his own bank by round-about processes which kept his name entirely out of' the business. Men of straw served him in this and disappeared after the trans. actions were completed. He was now only to be identified with these businesses according to the shares which he had openly purchased. The Melbourne branch, which had just been started, went with the rest of the property. Now, as Stephen Belwood took care to keep his own name out of the board of directors and be only an ordinary shareholder, he managed to keep clear of any of the future arrangements. This made a vast difference to his late employes, particularly' to his Melbourne manager, Walter 'Gordon, alias George Walters, and his friend Solomon Davies, alias Saul Denvers. These worthies no sooner had installed themselves, expecting a good time of it, than they found their wings clipped and themselves under the control of - strange masters. This they both resented bitterly, and in six months after their arrival at the Antipodes, they resigned, and were once more voyaging homewards, brooding upon their wrongs and plotting revenge. "If the boss tries to kick we'll tackle the Duchess," said the ex-manager, who knew a little more about affairs than even Stephen was aware. "He isn't the man to work a game like this with- out written authority, and he is the last man to destroy the incriminating documents. I'll work to get those from him, and then we can have two cows to milk, Sol, my boy." While these things were happening, however, the long expected cablegram arrived from Dawson City. The Duchess had written in cypher, and the message had been wired from Vancouver. On the strength of this Stephen Belwood did a stroke worthy of his reputation. He had rumours spread so as to make a slump in Klondike, then he pur- chased privately as many shares as he could. The . following week, all the papers were filled with the discovery of Sir Lewis Eastlake and his com- panions, and the highest boom of the century was mu full force. Mr. Stephen Belwood and those in his confidence were, in a week's time, fabulously rich, besides being roasters of the position. Whe.n he received time wire from his agents in Melbourne of the resignation and departure of his rebellions tools, he experienced a slight sensation of uneasiness, but he quickly recovered his calm- ness. - _ _

One morning in the month of March, as Mr. Belwood entered his city office, shivering under Ida fur overcoat, his chalky face more like a &,i-pse's than •ever, his dark eyes dim and deeply set, his walk ieehle and wearied, he saw in the outer office his former manager, Mr. Walter Gordon. Ile expected him to come about this time, therefore he did not express any surprise. He merely wished him good morning and ushered him into his private den. When the door was shut he removed his overcoat and sank weariedly into his chair. "Well, Walter, you did not care milt* for Haustralia surely, since you har back so quickly ? " "No, governor, I didn't like Australia, neither did Sol Davies, so we've come back like this proverbial--" "The voyage 'as done you good. You look splendid," said Stephen, eyeing him furtively. can't say the same for you, governor. You are not looking at all well." "Oh, I'm well henough. There's nothink hever much wrong with me." He waved his hand impatiently. He did not like people to condole with him about his personal appearance or apparent state of health. Walter Gordon knew this and grinned. "Well, I've come to consult with you about my future, Mr. Belwood. What do you propose ? " "Hi hexpect I must do something for han hold pal. Hi've been thinking h,)ver you and Sol Davies, hand I think I can fix yo i; hup." In what way?" -

"Well, I ha'nt got time to-day to discuss the :natter, has I 'ave some business to hattend in the City, but if you can come hout to my private 'ouse to-night we'll talk hit hover. Come habout ten ertlock hand 'aye a sUlo4e."

CHAPTER XXXIII.

FAREWELL TO VALERIE.

The wound which stretched Sir Lewis senseless on the deck of the Gadfly was not a serious one. It might have been, however, from the part it struck, with a thin-skulled man. Sir Lewis, however, possessed a good hard and solid frontal brain-pan, while the bullet did a ricochet, and made only an ugly-looking scalp wound. _

It drove -all Consciousness from him until the doctor had cut his hair and sewed up the broken skin. It also made him a very gory spectacle, and gave him the appearance of being the most desper- ately wounded man on the yacht. But after the blood was sponged from his face , and neck, and the doctor had revived him, he was able to go up on deck again and enjoy a cigar, while he listened to the news. The Duchess had been well-nigh frantic until he appeared and re- assured her that he was still alive and able to kick. Ten hours of sound sleep restored him almost to his former fine condition of health, aid the Duchess ' once again became serene. They had fought bravely and calmly, as Britisher, always do when attacked by long odds. It is only when the enemy are about of equal number that our courtrymen have a chance of being conquered, for we are a phlegmatic lot, and require something sudden and critical to rouse us up. Captain Eric St. Arnoul finished his career as he would have wished. After a good hour of hard shooting, during which he sent the souls of many desperadoes before him to the happy hunting- gronnds, he received his own instant despatch with a bullet between the eyes. There was no ricochet for him. The ball passed clean through his head and left but few traces of its passage ; when found, he was smiling as peacefully as a child does some- time in his sleep. These untimely deaths, with the wounded on their hands, cast a shadow of gloom for a time for the festive party. However, by the time they reached Yokohama, the dead were forgotten, and most' of the wounded convalescent. Then they went once again happily and cheerfully upon their way. It was at Port Said that Sir Lewis received the letters which told him that the divorce had been carried successfully and without appeal through the oourt. He was now able to announce to his friends his engagement to the Duchess, and the services of the Bishop were at once requisitioned. Alexandria, being a more fashionable resort for English society people than Said, the wedding was decided to be performed there. .It was a satisfactory occasion for the dowagers, as well as for the Bishop, for three of the most eligible young convalescents had been nursed by their inantoratas up to the marrying point. They decided to be tied at the same time as Sir Lewis and the Duchess Valerie, and accompany them on their honeymoon. Honeymoons generally are, as they ought to be, engrossing times, when the matches are made by Dan Cupid, particularly during the growing half of the honeymoon. It is the waning that has to be provided for. There was no waning for the Duchess Valerie. She felt that she could never merge from the passionate lover into the tolerant matron. She was a being of thrilling nerve and wild intensity. Her lover was now her husband, and his kisses removed all memory of those past miseries, hatreds, and guilt. She had entered the house of sin and falsehood to win him; therefore, she could never regain her lost estate, but with love she could forget. In his arms she remembered her evil no more. - - _

He was an honourable gentleman; therefore, she had no fear for his fidelity. He would always he true to the vows he had taken at Alexandria—that she knew and felt satisfied about. Marriage is heaven to a wife who laves, so long as her soul has no room for doubt or jealousy. Jealous Valerie was, and over would be of his past, but it was a jealousy which made her the more eager to please him, which kept her affections more active. It was a jealousy that crushed and silenced the voice of conscience, and made her his devoted slave. But although so dotingly and passionately fond of this not over brilliant gentleman, she was woman of the world enough—young as she still was—to take care not to weary him with her fondness. 6ir Lewis. or as he was termed now, his Grace the Due di Idumea—for Valerie had her titles as well as her other possessions to bestow upon this favoured child of fortune—liked travelling, and he had no desire yet to return to England; therefore they went from place to place during the whole of that winter and well on to the next spring. Valerie had been permitted to take a deeper draught from the fountain of love than fall to the lot of ordinary humanity; yet still the fountain flowed on as lavishly as ever. 'Would it never cease. to flow ? _

"Never!" she vowed to herself, as she stooped to the rill and drank thirstily. "When love begins to ebb, or run lower, I shall go on the turn of the tide." She did not expect it to last for ever any more than she could expect her youth and loveliness to endure; but she resolved to end it herself long before that time. "I must always be young in his eyes; always beautiful, always his lover." She bad some years before her yet, however, she thought, and sufficient for the day was the pleasure to be compressed into it. So she loved, and was loved in the way her spirit craved for, all the time being prepared for the end. _ _ .

Their movements, of course, were chronicled in all the fashionable and halfpenny democratic papers. The upper and under classes both demand to know what the privileged ones of the earth are doing. In the month of March it was announced that they had talon un their abode in Ploreuot. .

To Florence tile messenger oi evil, 4cllonion Davies, was now opeeding as fast as trains cold take him.

Valerie was out driving with her husband when Solomon called, and sent in his card with his new name engraved upon it. He was superciliously told to call ag .in, but he belonged to those tribes which do not resent insults and never miss present opportunities. He had no time to waste on useless calls. He had brought with him from London the latest edition of one of the evening papers in which the death of Stephen Belwood was announced, also the arrest of Walter Gordon. Dark hints were given of some startling coming disclosures of scandal in high life, as the result of the financier's sudden death, and he wanted to be the first to warn the Duchess. Therefore, finding he could not get admittance to the villa, he sat down on the steps of the balcony and waited calmly.

• • I 0

Solomon Davies has been paid a handsomer price than he expected for his timely information, and while he is hurrying to the bank to get the open cheque cashed, the Duchess Valerie sits alone in her boudoir. Her moment for decision had arrived; but it was not love that was on the wane. It was the total destruction of love, respect, and every- thing she prized which had come upon her out of the blue. She knew her husband too well to think that he would condone her offences. He would loathe her for her cruelty as he would abhor him- self for his credulity. The end had come. How delicious those months had:been, but ah! how short they seemed now that they were over! "If I only had been allowed three days of warning!" she muttered. "Ali, how I have wasted this past week!" She had poison beside her—a swift-killing potion which would carry her out of life in an instant of time; but she had not bade adieu to her love. Could she dare wait and do so first?

She read the paragraph once more. The police evidently had been reticent as yet, and the probing reporter had just got a hint and no more. With Stephen Belwood dead and Walter Gordon in prison, she was able to read between the lines, with the key supplied to her by Davies; but would her husband yet? No! If he had only this information to guide him, she was safe until the next day's issue, at any rate. "I shall not do it yet," she exclaimed pas- sionately. "I shall wait till my darling returns, then I shall know by his face if he knows more than this report can tell him. 0 God of Heaven, give me but one more day and night of life and love and I ask no more."

It was an impious prayer, yet it was granted to her, as impious prayers sometimes are. From a secret drawer in her writing-desk she took a tiny box which contained only one large capsule. This box she placed in her purse, and then she went downstairs to wait and watch for her husband. She was now ready for him ;—and death. She had not long to wait, for her husband had seen the account of the financier's death, and hastened home at once to tell her about it. He had not read the other paragraphs. She knew it as soon as she saw him, and her heart throbbed with a fierce delight and gratitude. His face was slightly graver than usual, but the same trusting and eager light lay in his blue eyes. "I fear we shall have to go to England, Valerie," he said as he entered.

"Yes,, and why, Lewis ? " she asked as calmly as she could.

"1 have Frs, itow read about the death of Stephen Belwood from heart disease. He was found dead in his study two days ago, and expect, as director and partner in the Yukon Mines, 1 shall be wanted."

"Very well, dear, we will go to-morrow.. "Oh! divine joy! Ah, merciful God, she , inwardly cried, 'thanks for this blessed respite." Outwardly she said gleefully: "Our last day in sweet Italy. What shall we do with it, Lewis ? " "Oh, go for another long drive, and to the opera to-night, that's about the best thing we can do, unless you have other engagements." "No, I wish to be romantic to-day and to-night. I want you all to myself, sweet love. Our engage- ments—bah! Let us cancel them all, and pass to- day and to-night as lovers. Let us shut the gates against all visitors for the next twenty-four hours, and try to feel as if this was our last day together. Say, Lewis, what would you do if it was our last day together? " "Don't, Valerie. It gives me the blues to think of such things. It would be too—too awful for words or for realisation."

"But, supposing a prophet came and said: 'To- morrow, Valerie will be no more,' how would you propose to spend the day ? " She laughed lightly, yet she leaned against his breast and wafted her sweet breath into his face.

"If I thought it at all possible that We were to part to-morrow I should pass the intervening hours holding you to my heart." "Then let us pass our time that way," she answered. , "Let's stay within our walls, have our lunch in the arbour, and linger there until the nightingales begin to sing. It is full moon to- night. Let us go out to-night after dinner and watch the moon, and while you smoke your cigar and drink your brandy and Friedricksall, I shall play my lute and sing to you. as Juliet might have done had.she kept house with Romeo." There was something about Valerie when she played the siren, as she was doing now, that no man could possibly resist. Her programme was quickly decided upon. It was the first time since the wedding that Valerie had suggested, or appeared to suggest anything to him, therefore he yielded all the more readily. Ah rare Valerie, discreetest of rives. He had never moVed a step during the past six months without your artlessly artful suggestions.

The next morning they came down to breakfast together, lovers still. A pile of letters lay arranged at each plate. "Am I to he tyrant still for one hour longer, taro wzio " asked Valerie, with an affectation of stage majesty. "You know it wants one hour to complete the twenty-four of my empire ? " "Yes, what are your orders, Queen?" replied and asked Lewis, casting a hungry look upon his letters.

"That you wait until my hour of supremacy has sounded before you read a single letter unless it be from me."

"On one condition." "No conditions if you recognise our compact; yet let me hear what your condition is ? " "That you don't read yours either." "I promise you that Lewis, my slave for one more hour."

They sat down to breakfast. She was bright- eyed and beautiful, and he could not resist kissing her twice while she poured out the coffee. She laid down jaw coffee-pot each time and responded to his kisses with fervour.

After breakfast was over she stood over him and said tenderly, and, as he thought at the time, a little sadly: "One more kiss, darling, the last for this my twenty-four hours of empire, then I shall go and write you a little love-letter, which you must read before you open one of these on the table. Will you promise?" "Yes, Valerie, I can promise you that much, for lam now going to smoke a cigar on the terrace." He drew her into his arms and kissed her on the brow, the cheeks, and the mouth. For a long moment she lay against his heart, then she kissed him again and left him. She said nothing as she went out of the door, she merely looked back, smiling on him with that graceful pose that was her own, and which no other woman could imitate, and wafted him a farewell kiss.

CHAPTER XXXIV.

THE END. -RELIGHTED FIRES.

During the weeks when Hope was fighting fot her infant's life, he read no papers, consequently she had no knowledge of what excited the attention of the world for a few days, and which was of c much personal interest to her. The suicide of the Duchess Valerie was the biggest sensatiOn. After that, the publication of those incriminating instructions, which she had at different times sent to Stephen Belwood. The Duchess, like most of her sex, did not pause to consider when she had a pen in her white hand and a sheet of paper before .her. Stephen Belwood had kept those letters all in order, not for the publicity which had come to them, but merely as a hold over his fair employer _

Walter Gordon rem'aried to his solicitor: "It is just like my luck to be arrested for a trifle like this, while these letters were on me."

A's for this trifle, which had been the giving of a bogus cheque in payment of a small account on the eve of his departure from England, he might have squared that. But the letters caused an explosion, and sent Walter Gordon and the pawnbroker's assistant, who had committed perjury, on a five years' term of penal servitude. Pretty, romantic, Mrs. Algernon Churchill Champion. manazed to escape with two years for

Nir part or toe conspiracy. lie swore that her husband knew nothing about it, and the jury had to believe her. Mr. Champion felt grateful to his little wife for screening him. He knew, of cottrt. that her motive was not entirely clovution, but nO matter, it saved his nlnie and credit in the City. Hope in that quiet village, and confined to the sick chamber, had no means of learning how her name had once more been dragged into the court and cleared. At this time, even that tardy justice would not have moved her much.

Not so much as did the verdict of the doctor, when he gave her the blessed assurance that her baby was out of danger. He had fluctuated between life and death, so close at times to the fatal line that she could hardly believe in the.good tidings. It took weeks, however, even after this, before Lewis John could be removed to allow that sick room to be disinfected. When Hope could find time to read a newspaper the death of Valerie was, forgotten. Walter Gordon had done the first month of his time, and was congratulating himself that perjury and fraud were the only charges against him. Murder might have been easily added. It was, indeed, a fortunate matter for him that a night had passed before the body of Stephen Bel wood was discovered. Fortunate, also, that the doctor knew the condition of his heart and his drug-taking habits, and therefore regarded it as & natural death. He had one anxiety now to trouble him in this retreat—his cheque. He had not signed it, and it was kept with his other effects by the authorities until his sentence had expired. Would he be able to get it cashed then after five years? It all depended upon what money was in the bank by that time in the late Mr. Stephen Belwood's name. This was a corroding care to. this undiscovered murderer. Another dark medita- tion kept him in a constant fury, and that was the escape of Mr. Solomon Davies. He knew that Solomon must have seen the Duchess before her death. It maddened him to think about the rewards she must have given Solomon for his warn- ing and that theywere at large while he was shut up.° True, he had given Solomon the message to go; but that was before his conviction, it had not entered his mind that the Duchess would poison herself about such a trifle. He expected her to take a trip somewhere, and use her influence in his behalf. Walter Gordon could not understand people with title and wealth taking their lives. The uncertainty about his cheque, and the whereabouts of the Davieses gnawed at his vitals constantly. One warm forenoon, about the latter half of May, Hope sat with her baby boy in the porch taking the sun. Lewis John was entirely better now, but he was not quite so rosy as ho had been before his illness. His colour was coming back, but it was coming slowly. All at once a shadow fell on the baby's face, and glancing up, Hope saw the man who had been her husband. His eyes were fixpd on the child, so that he gave her time to recover herself. How glad she felt now that she had kept up her disguise. Re would consider her only as a servant, for doubtless he had come to see hie child.

How changed he was, from the light-hearted, reckless and improvident husband who had parted from her at Cherbourg! Was it two or twenty-two years ago? she wondered, as she watched hii. furtively, waiting for him to speak. fie was grey-faced, and his light hair seemed to have become withered; in his fair moustache also were a. number of silver hairs. A deep and frown— ing furrow lay between his eyebrows; but it was his mouth and his eyes that appalled that watcher. The mouth was grim, and the eyes were coldly stern. He appeared like a man who had parte& with his yoP•th and all its aspiration and dreams. He was as one disillusionised.

AI last he spoke without looking at the mother: "What is the name of this child ? " • Stern, abrupt, and gloomy were the ton. He used to be polite and gentle to his inferiors. He seemed to have lost that quality now. "Lewis John Eastlake, answered Hope, faintly. He started, and looked keenly at her. She had not been able to disguise her voice nor her eyes. She knew in an instant that he had recognised her before even he said: "Hope—and here ? " _ _

"Where should a mother be, if not beside her ailing child 2" she asked steadily, as she rose and ushered him into the parlour. Mrs. Hambly had joined them, hearing the voices, and Hope gave the baby to her to hold.

"Kindly excUse-me for a few moments, Sir Lewis Eastlake; I shall join you presently." He bent his head, and she retired, to get rid et that hateful disguise. Whatever the consequences were to be, she resolved to have no more conceal- ments. She would appca,l to his generosity, .and try to be permitted to keep her (kid. That was her one thought now—to be allowed to keep her baby; yet she felt a great pity for thus. miserable man whom she had once loved so dearly. While she was making her toilet, Sir Lewis East- lake sat with his head resting on his hands and his eyes fixed on the floor. One image was in his brain only, and there it stood perpetually before him—the image of the Duchess as she had parted with him at the door of the breakfast-room at Florence.

Hope soon appeared as he had known her, only with her hair somewhat shorter. He knew the cause of those shortened tresses, as he looked at her with tender pity, as she had thought about him. She learnt front his lips- all that the papers might have told her had she seen them, and a warm glow crept into her heart at the thought of hee innocence and purity being established. Her boy would not have to blush for his mother—that was good. She thanked God that this goodness had been given to her; it wiped away all her sufferings. "I have sought you everywhere, Hope, since the death of—Valerie, he said.

"You knew where to find your child, though, did you not, Lewis ? " she asked gently. "Yes; but I wished to find you first." "Then you ought to have come here, without searching further," she answered calmly. "We must be married again, •Hoe—for the sake of the child. Will you take me? " "Yes, Lewis, I must—for the sake of the child,' she replied gently. So the bargain was made between the pair, who had once lighted their fires together from the torch of Eros.

Four weeks afterwards Hope stood beside her former husband at the altar, and they were re- united with much pomp and ceremony. _ _ _ _

As the bride toOk her vows, she -seemed to see her baby boy stretching ont his arms and blessing her. As the bridegroom took his, in front of his vision floated the dead Valerie, as she had last said farewell, just before that awful discovery which had blanched his hair and seared his soul.

Hope, who, had been Lady Eastlake before, was' now a Duchess—by the gift of her dead rival. Her baby, by the same gift, was a Marquis; baby Marquis, with a big work cut out for him— the uniting of two severed hearts, and the exorcising. of the beautiful phantom which stood at present between them.

The phantom which now refused to be banished' from each memory; the phantom which only thsb. child wizard would be able to exorcise in time, perhaps, and bring joy and rest to those two un. happy and haunted spirits. Would Valerie ever die and drift away? Truly; as yet. he had not been baulked even by death of her Avenge.

[Tam Fan.]

WOMEN'S CHAT."

There seems no longer any doubt that the German Emperor is to pay a visit to this country. As at present announced he is to arrive at Windsor Castle on Novem- ber 20th, where he will stay for several days as the guest of the Queen. It is several years since his Imperial Majesty wag last over here, and since then he has dis- tinctly progressed in the estimation of the world. This is certainly the general view in this country, quite apart from the tact that we have long since forgotten the Kaiser's interference in a matter of politics in a quarter which is just now the cynosure of public interest. The Vienna papers confirm the announcement that the widowed Crown Princess Stephanie is to be marriect in London on November 22nd to Count Ellemer Lonyay. The Count is the nephew of the late Hungarian Prime Minister, and has been some years in the Austrian diplo- matic service. It was while he was secretary of the Austro-Hungarian Embassy in London, that the acquain- tance with the Princess began ; he attending her during her stay in this country three years ago. The Crown Princess is of course still young, being only thirty six and Count Lonvay is two years older. It is obviously a love match, but though the marriage, which is to take place at the Austro-Hungarian Embassy is a misalliance, it is understood that the aged Emperor has given his consent, on condition that the Princess Stephanie resigns all the rights and privileges which she acquired as the wife of the heir apparent. The Princess will cease to be a member ot the Imperial [lase, or of Imperial rank, and she also gives up the greater Dart of her iecome,hor allewence in future being £8,300 a year instead of £33,000. Loneen still keeps full, and dances and dinners are in full swing. But these gaieties are not ail as real as they appear on the surface and despite society's rule that one must wear a smiling face even if one's heart is breaking; anxiety is plainly written on many ot the faces one sees just now. The next news may be " another great slaughter," and a loved one's name may be found on the lists of the wounded--or dead. Still anything is better Ithan sitting brooding, waiting for news, and so all sorts of entertainments are being gut op, and attended,by the anxious ones, who long for the days and nights to pass, and yet dread each batch of news as it comes to band. The weather too has not been good to us. One looks of course for fogs in November but in October one hopes for better things. 01 late however, it has been damp and deprersing to a degree, and the incessant fogs and mists, calculated indeed to try the strongest amerg us. What to wear has been a problem difficult to solve. Furs have proved too warm ,autumn coats and capes scarcely cosy enough, while early autumn wraps have had to be passed by altogether. Skirts are now much has scanty about the hipe,which is an item to be teankful for. It will soon be possible to sit down quite cemfortably and without fear of dis- aster—in the shape of splitting seams. Dress sleeves are still as small as they well can be. Soon will came reaction in this quarter also, when we shall, in all probability once more wend our steps in the direction of the balloon variety. Coat sleeves are all being built with a dart or two at the top, to lift them slightly out- wards. The newest tailor made skirts are built with the front breadth only lined. All fabrics just now are fairly 'thick and heavy, and as silk slips are worn any further lining is considered unnecessary. Of course the cloth or tweed hangs and clings better, which is an all important item at thesmement. A story which reads rather like a fairy tale, but which seems to be authenticated comes from a suburb of Lon- don where aMr Collins recently purchased a parcel of books at an auction, and among them an old family Bible. Mrs. Collins fcund several of the pages pasted together, and of course, wanted to separate them. This having been done. six £5 notes were discovered, with a letter dated 1840, stating that the owner of the notes, having no heir, desired to leave them to the person who might own the Bible. Possibly the incident may result in an increased de- mand for old family Bibles, but however that may be, the story remind us of an expedient to which Napoleon I is said to have resorted in ccnsequence of the reluctance of the public to accept five franc pieces. The Emperor gave it to be understood that he had ordered a cheque for £4,000 to be concealed in one of these Coins, and it its stated that thereafter there was a great demand for them, thcusands being split in two in the hope of find- ing the cheque. But nobody succeeded in discovering it,and perhaps there was a very good reason why failure attended the various e,says in that direction. There is cue fact which goes very far to explain the ravings of a section of the French press with respect to this country. Whenever there are Mf mbers of a pro- fession engaged in a struggle for sixpences. one finds a low standard of honour, and an equally low standard of lefficiency, but in France there must be many news- papers which are engaged in a struggle for sous. Paris alene has ne fewer than 2,685 joernals, of which 152 are published daily, and in the departments there are 4,000 more, of which 355 are daily. In Louden, with a much larger population, there are 460 newspapers, of which about 30 are dailies. Even this number is too many with the ever increasing tendency to resort to pure sensationalism. The war news of coarse offers a fine opening, and before hostilities were commenced, there had teen no lack of "groat battles." But they were mostly on paper, and the void was filled by those e ten- to:eau hawkers who make night hideous in the streets of the Metropolis. Every householder who has a sliding gaselier is au are of the fact that when the water in the tube falls suffi- ciently low, by evaporation, the gas will escape into the room. As a rule, people have no regular tiese to attend to these matters, they generally wait for the bubbling noise, which the escaping gas usually causes, before putting in fresh water. The need for this constant attention can be obviated, as cue of the medical papers reminds its readers,by the addition of a little thick oil— such as linseed which floating on the top of the water, will prevent its evaporation. The same journal sitters a warning against sleeping in bedrooms situated over sitting rooms, the air of which is vitiated by gas jets and other gases. These it is said, find their way tin ouch the thin porous ceilings and effect the air in the sleeping apartment above. The remedy it le added, is to have the bAroonss on tbe ground floor, but the evil—if it is anything more than a theoretical evil—can be sufficiently well dealt with by the less revolutionary methods of attending to the proper ventilation of rooms throughout the house.

NOTlCE.—Solvers who become entitled to a prize in this comp3tition can, if they desire, have any book on the game of Draughts equal to the value of 53. instead of cash prize, by notifying the Draughts Editor.

In connection with the above competition we may state that we offer a monthly prize of 5s to the competitor who succeeds in placing himself at the head of the Solvers' List at the end of the month. Where the prize is awarded the winner's score is cancelled, while the remaining competitors' points go towards the next month's competition. The win- ner may re-enter the competition if he chooses commencing again at the bottom of the list. Points are awarded as follows: Two for the correct solu- tion of a problem, two for showing a dual eolution, two for correcting the author's terms, and one for each minor correction.

—•— Intending competitors may commence at any time.

NEWS OF THE GAME. The second annual meeting of the English Draughts Association is to be held to-day (Satur- day), at the Sultan Cafe, Cheapside, London, at six p.m. The business to be transacted, besides the election of officers, is of much importance, as several new rules have been proposed. Of these perhaps the most important is that bearing upon the time limit,whish at present is five minutes per move, The motion to amend this stands in the name of Mr. E. L. Benson,the London international, who proposes to substitute 20 moves per hour. For many years this change has been advocated, but only in the case of a New York club has it been put into practice. How far it may be acceptable to the players likely to compete in the next English tournament we are unable to say, and,after all,they are. most concerned. Theoretically, the rule is an excellent one, and if adopted may be expected to raise the standard of cross-board play. Like every- thing elseit is open to abuse, but on the whole its , advantages should outweigh its drawbacks.- -The president, Mr. Dunne, proposes to change the system of playing the heats in the championship tournament. As the rule stands, six games are to be balloted from " Robertson's Guide." Mr. Dunne suggests instead four unrestricted games, and a ballot for the other two from the openings in "Robertson's Guide." The tendency at present is towards restriction. The existing rule worked well in the Liverpool tournament in keeping the heats within reasonable limit. Time is a great con- sideration in these tournaments—alike for the players and the officials.—A new rule, proposed by ' Mr. A: Hynd, of Manchester, is likely to meet with some opposition. It prohibits all members of the association from publishing a game, or pert of a game, without proper sanction, under the penalty of expulsion. So drastic a rule is surely not required to atop the one or two pressmen who, wittingly or otherwise, have transgressed in this respect. It is a moot point whether games played in public, and a charge made for admission, can be made copy- right, Mr. Hynd's rule aims only at members, and leaves ordinary spectators alone.—" Liverpool Mercury." DEATH Or LEES, THE ANALYST.—We regret to announce the death of Mr. James Lees, the world- famous analyst. He had been ailing for several month, but latterly hopes were entertained of his speedy recovery. Unfortunately he had a relapse, and passed peacefully away on Thursday morning at his mother's residence at Dalmellington. He was 38 years of age, and had devoted the greater part of his short life to the study of the game of draugbts. His "Guide " to the pastime is a most pcpular work. He was thoroughly posted up on all the games, and had amassed an astonishing amount of play, the most of which would no doubt have seen the light within the next few years had he lived. For the last four or five years he acted as the games editor of "The Draughts World" and "The Draughts Quarterly," a couple of admirable publications. His work for these periodicals was characterised by great accuracy and conscientiousness. Notwithstand- ing his indifferent health he fulfilled his duties al- most up till the day of his death. Mr. Lees was a highly intelligent man and well read in general literature. His death is a great loss to the world of draughts.—" Glasgow Herald."

The small diagram will enable our readers to understand the notation by which the moves of the game are recorded. Black ccuples the squares num- bered 1 to 12 and white those numbered 21 to 32. The figures thus 11-15 Indicate that a move is made from and to the squares so numbered. Black has the opening move and the rairs of figures apply alternately to Black and White to the close.

THY ILLNESS OF THE REV. W. J. WOODS, B.A.

The Uv. W. J. Woods, 8.A., the Secretary of the Congregational Union, will have the sym- pathy of many friends in all parts of the country in his continued illness. Mr. Woods was very poorly during his recent stay on the other side of the Atlantic. and has come home in a serious condition of health. The November Free Church Chrom'cle states that his doctor has perepnp- torily ordered him to keep away from his office at the Memorial Hall, London, for the next three months. We trust that a long period of repose will restore Mr. Woods to his usual health. His geniality, wisthnn, and ahility have been very valuable to the Congregational body. THE CHURCH CONGE,ESS AND NONCONFORMITY.

The discussion at the Congress en the rela- tions between the Anglican Church and the Nonconformists has attracted great attention from the latter. The tone of the speakers was as friendly as could be desired. Dr. Henry Waco, formerly Principal of King's College, re- minded i‘rethren that in England certainly one-third—and probably one-half—of profess- ing Christians repudiated the Anglican Church. in the "Free Church handbook." to which Dr. Waco made repeated reference, the rela- tive statistics are fully set forth. Taking in the whole English-speaking world, Dr. Wace found something less than three and a-half millions of Anglican communicants, as com- pared with considerably more than seventeen million Free Church members. Nonconformists are certainly not disposed to boast of their strength. They know too well that it is not great enough for the work they are called on to do. But they feel that their numerical preponderance over the whole world entitles them to the respect and consideration of their Anglicrrn friends. They do not believe in a national establishment of religion, and they can make no truce with sacerdotalism, but, barring these restrictions, they desire to co- operate with their brethren of the establish- ment in the evangelisation of England.

THE MISSIONERS OF THE NATIONAL FEE'

CHURCH COUNCIL.

We hear that " Gipsy " Smith and, Mr. IV. R. Lane, the missioners of the National Free Church Council, continue their work with great success. It may not be generally known that Mr. Lane was formerly in the Grenadier Guards. With regard to the former, Bromsgrove and Burton-on-Trent have been the scenes of his most recent lectures. Mr. Smith is a true natural orator. He describes his message as "New Testament Religion in earnest. He eschews the sensational, and studies to be quiet. He avoids anything that approaches vulgarism, and there is no striving after effect. "The Story of Gipsy Smith's Career," a penny brochure, has just been published by Mr. Thomas Law, of the Memorial Hall, London. There are some rather good stories in the booklet. For in- stance:

===== 0002601_18991027_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
A FORTNIGHT'S NOTICE TO THE DEVIL.

The Gipsy has had many strange dealings with men. There was a case, at once sad and comical, at Chatham. A poor ignorant man— very ignorant—attended the services regularly for weeks. (The Gipsy was connected at the time with the Salvation Army, or as it was then called, "The Christian Mission.") One night, as he was turning out, he said to the Gipsy, "I am fifty years of age, and I've served the devil all the time. But I'm giving him a fort- night's notice." The Gipsy reasoned with him, and urged immediate decision. "Oh, no," said the poor man, "I would not like to be treated like that myself. lam going to do to others as I would like to be done by. But I've given the devil a fortnight's notice." When a week had passed, as the poor tellow was again passing out of the hall, he held up one finger to signify that the devil had just one week longer Jf him. When the notice had expired, the devil was dismissed, and the man who had been in his service for fifty years entered a service which be liked much better, and which he has never left. He became a true and humble dis- ciple of another Master."

THE RAILWAY COMPANIES AND ELEMENTARY

EDIGTATION.

The November Free Church Chronicle, of which we have seen an early copy, contains a brief lalt important article on this subject. It appears that in one very populous district of .Pcter borough the only elementary schools are the schools built by the Great Northern Rail- wv Company. Recently the Company granted a lease of the schools to the vicar of the parish on the condition that they should he conducted as the Church Schools of the parish. Now, the General Committee of the National Free Church Council (of which the Free Church Chronicle is the official organ) consider that this constitutes a grievance to Nonconformists. There are many sturdy Non- conformists among the shareholders of the Company, and it is certain that if they knew of this they would oppose it. The shareholders include men of all creeds, and if the Company chooses to let its schools pass out of its hands, it is felt that they ought to pass under popular control. The Chronicle has many other inte- resting features, including a character sketch of Dr. Barrett, of Norwich; an article on "Th. Next Step in Temperance Reform," by the Rev. C. F. Aked, the well-known Baptist minister of Liverpool; a sketch of the Mayor of Hull, who is a Free Churchman and a local.preacher ; and a large supply of fresh, interesting paragraphs about the Free Church Councils and Free Church life generally.

===== 0002601_18991027_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONGREGATIONAL UNION AT BRISTOL.

The meetings were a decided success. The number of delegates was the largest on record, no fewer than 1300 being accommodated. The Rev. H. Arnold Thomas, M.A., Chairman, who has ministered in Bristol for many years, gave an address to a crowded audience in the Broad- nead Chapel on "The Value of Religion." One who heard it, described it as "a poem on the Divine life in the soul." When Dr. Guinness Rogers rose to speak on the Twentieth Century Fund, he was accorded an ovation. His address was marvellous for its energy and vigour. Haif- a-million guineas is the sum aimed at, and it seems almost certain now that they will be got. Indeed, all the Twentieth Century Funds are likely to be successful. The Free Churches mean to start the new cen- tury with a well-filled war chest.

THE NEW PRESBYTERIAN COLLEGE AT

CAINIBUIDGE.

The Presbyterian Church of England has removed its theological seminary from London to Cambridge. The Presbyterians of England, as of Scotland, have always perceived the value and necessity of an educated ministry. They hope and believe that a famous Uni- versity town will supply a better atmosphere for the cultivation of theological learning than London. The College was opened free of debt —a circumstance which is a great personal triumph for Dr. John Watson ("an Mac- laren ) convener of the College Committee. A few weeks before the opening ceremony £16,000 was still wanted, but Dr. Watson toiled tremenduously, proved himself a cham- pion beggar, and had the satisfaction at an in- augural ceremony of being able, in his cha- racter as Convener of the College Committee, to look the whole world in the face, for he owed no man anything. The total sum raised- 16,000—has not been the gift of a few wealthy persons; every congregation, almost every member, gave something.

CHILDREN'S LEAGUE OF PITY.

In connection, with the Warrington Branch of the Children's League of Pity—an institution affili- ated with the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children—a meeting was held in the Town Hall on Btsturday afternoon.—Miss C. Broad- bent,the hon. secretary, stated that the Warrington branch was started on December 4th, 1813, when 55 children joined,and now there were 77 Leaguers. Since that date, £176 5s had been contributed by the Leaguers, except abut £4B whioh had been realised by sales, &c, hel?, in Warrington for the purpose. A branch at Frodsham was founded on November 11th, 189 g. There were now 58 Leaguers, and £22 17s 81 had been contributed up to the present, The Altrincham branch wee com- menced on October 15th,1897. Now it had 63 mem- bers,and £B9 12s 8d had been realised since that date. —Miss Bolton, the assistant secretary of the enciety stated that the founder of the society was once a boy named "Cherry Cheeks," who was new known I as Benjamin Waugh. Some children would perhaps remark that they were too small to help. That was not right, as no child was too small to help them in their work. They could put away each week perhaps, a half-penny, which would mount up at the end of the year. They, in Warrington, with their £l7B which they had contributed, since the commencement of the branch, had been the means of helping to make over 200 little children happy. They had been the means of introducing peace and happiness into their lives. The work of the society was to make little children happy in their own homes. There were now about 7,000 members in the society,and they hoped that in a ehort time that number would be increased to 40,000.

NEW POLICE STATION. Foundation-stone Laying at Warrington.

An important event in the history of Warrington occurred on Tuesday when the Mayor of the Borough (Alderman J. E. Wright) conducted the ceremony of laying the foundation-stone of new police beildings to be erected at a cost of £22,000. The existing police station in Irlarn-street has long been considered inadequate for the needs of the town and the accomm-sdatiin of a force of over GO men. Sir Herbert Croft, tier .alajesty's Inspector, has been most insistent in his condemnation of the premises for years past, and the urgent representa- tions of the Government coupled with the danger of losing the grant,have prevailed upon the Corpor- ation to at once take in hand the important and costly 'work of providing new buildings suit- able for all present and many future require- ments. The site selected is that at the Bank Quay end of Wilson-Patten-street and which has since 1873 been covered by the militia storehouse of the 4tt, Ro al Lancashire Militia,the block of buildings being, bowever,better, known locally as " The old Barracks." It is an ad- mirable site for the purpose of either barracks or police station, the only objectien to it being that it IS Hot so central as the old premises in Irlam-street. It has the exceptional ad vantage of being bounded on three sides by streets, the fourth side abutting on the premises of the Patten Arms Hotel. The buildings will be ranged round the three open sides of the site, with a central wing projecting into the enclosed quadrangle, forming on plan somewhat the form of an E. The accommodation will comprise a completely equipped police station, with the entrance in the centre of the Arpley-street front, the cells being placed in the central wing under the Police Court, which occu- pies' the upper floor in this block. There will be a' detective department, rooms for the Chief Con- stable and Wetch Committee, in the block facing Wilson Patten-street, with coroner's court and magistrates' rooms, over the quarters for single con- stables, consisting of 20 dormitory cubicles, recrea- tion and mess rooms, sick room, &c., and a pair of semi-deteched residences for inspector and ser- geant, fronting on to Museum-street. A mortuary and post mortem block and 'ambulance house will also be provide]. The buildings will be for the most part two storeys in height, faced externally with a good red brick and terracotta dressings. The architectural treatment will be of a simple Renaissance character, a substantial and dignified effect being obtained without extravagance, the idea being to provide a building so arranged and equipped as to enable the work of maais- trates and police to be carried on in the most efficielt manner pesible at a minimum cost, consistent with its public character and the prominent site it occupies. The principal front will be to Wilson Patten-street, the higher central portion containing on the upper filer the coroner's court, lighted by large semi-circular windows under a bold projrcting cornice, being fl inked by two projecting bays carried up as dome-capped towers above the adjoining roofs. The magistrees entrance will occupy a central position is this front, and consist of a columnar porch,carrying one of the flanking bays. The Arpley-street and Museum-st. fronts will be simpler in character. The forjner will be designed with oriel and gable in the centre over the police station entrance, and with a range of projecting pedimental oriels on either side. The public entrance b the courts will also be in this front near the police entrance. The entrance to the constables' quarters will be from Mueerm-street, as will also the doors of the inspector's and sergeant's houses. Large gates will give access to the central open Icourt from both Wilson Patten-street and Museum-street. A foreco rt about 151 t. wide :enclosed by posts and chains, will encircle the buildings. The design was I selected in open competition and the work is being learned out under the control of the architect, Mr. R. Burns Dick, of the firm of Messrs. Cackett and Burns Dick, Newcestle-on-Tyne. The contractor is a well- known local builder, namely, Mr. C. W. Davenport, of Stockton Heath, while the clerk of the works is Mr. Henry Hallard (Liverpool). The ceremony of laying th& foundation stone of such an important and costly building is not a ceminon one in any town, and it is quite a rarity in Warrineten, which does not boast of a plentitude of municipal build- ings of any great size or recent date. Occasion was taken to make the event to some extent memorable by ; conducting the ceremony with considerable éclat. At 12 45 the members of the Corporation, officials, and ; guests, assembled at the Town Hall, from whence they , marched in procession, starting at one o'clock, to the te'te of the new buildings. A body of police under the control of the Chief Constable led the way, the Mayor's isword and mace bearers immediately preceding his worship. Then came the general company to the num- ' ber of nearly one hundred, the route being by way of Winmarleigh-street, arid Wilson Patten-street. The Mayo, was actomeanied by Alderman Sutton (Sen. Alderman) and Mr J Lyon Whittle (Town Clerk). , Then followed !Aldermen Francomb, Blackhurst, Roberts, Hutchinson I and Bolton ; Councillors Smethurst, Baird, Ellison, Lee IShillcock, John Roberts, John Crosfleld, Dr Cannell , Peacock, Woods. Bennett, Tinnion, Hepherd, Pierpoint Plumpton, F R Roberts, James Evans, Longton, Rogers I Jolley ;Dr Smith (police surgeon). Dr Gorman, (medical officer of health),Mr Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), , Mr John Fairhurat (borough treasurer), Mr W H Wood- cock, (assistant treasurer, Zec.), T Longdin (borough surveyor), John Sutcliffe (assistant borough surveyor), G F F White (deputy clerk to the magistrates), Jern es Dees (manager of the depots), Fred Taylor (secretary of the gas department), Charles Madelry (secretary, librarian and curator. C B Aylward (art master), IMr R Burns Dick (the architect), Mr Cackett (of the firm of Cackett and Burns Dick) ; Mr Bennett (president of the Lancashire Society of architects), Mr Gunson, (architect Manchester), Mr Hindle ; and Messrs John , White, James Ilepherd, George Pilling, R T Fairclough, W B Roberts John Geddes, J W Hayward, T Birchall, R G Hensball% W Owen, 8 Wright,vrlaile in waiting near the stone were the Mayoress (Mrs Wright and Miss Wright), Mr and Mrs T Birtles, Mr J Birtles, Mr W H Robinson, and Mr John Lunt. The day wan quite fine and bright,so that the proceedings were conducted amid the pleasantest of cenditions. When everybody had secured a pontion in the vicinity of the massive founda- tion-stone, _ _ _

_ Alderman SUTTON said they would all be aware that they had met there that afternoon for the purpose of witnessing the ceremony of laying the foundation stone of the tow police premises by the Mayor. Before c tiling upon him to perform the cerem.,ny it would perhaps, not be out of place if he gave a few particulars con- cerning the site el the new premises. The new police premises will etan,l on the site of the storehouse and quarters formeylv occupied by the 4th Royal Lancashire Militia. The Militia storehouse and premises were erected in 1873, hut were not occupied many years by the Militia. In 1883 the premises were offered for sale, being then tried as a school by the Guardians of the Warrington Union at a rent of £lBO a year. The entire site contains 7,738 square yards, and was purchased in 1895 by Mr. Alderman Fairclough for the sum of £4,750. IThe land is freehold, and cost less than 6d per yard without considering the value of the old materials. The Government Inspector has for many years been twat persistent in his demands on the Cor- poration for new police premises. o one for a moment would contend that the present premises in Iriam-strest were at all suitable or adequate for police purposes. The old premises were built under an old Act of Parliament obtained in 1813, which was "an Act for Paving and Improving the Town of War- rington and for building a new Bridewell in the town," and the present premises were erected long before the incorp.,ratien of the borou2h. It was well-known that the Corpcoation received out of moneys provided by Parliament one half the cost of pay and clothing of police which amounted last year to tie sum of nearly £3,000. This money was paid on a certificate of the Secretary of State that the Police Force has been efficiently main- taieed, and as cue or two towns have lost the grant through not building new premises when required to do co it became a case of Holson's choice about building the new police premises in Warringeon. WARRINGTON MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

I Four contests for municipal honours will take place next Wednesday. The compact of the Liberal and Conservative parties not to oppose retiring members of the Council has been observed, but con- tests have been forced in four welds by independent candidates. Three of these are nominees of the Ratepayers and Property Owners' Association, and the fourth has been brought forward by a new organisation in the town known as the Citizens' Union,which it opposed to the interference of politi- cal parties in citizenship Mr. John Pemh,:rton, of Winwick-street, is the candidate of this Union and he opposes the return of Councillor Jolley in Latch- ford ward. He claims to have received promises of support from Conservatives, Liberals and Socialists. In Bewsey Ward the return of Councillor J Shaw Green is challenged by Mr, Elijah Cunnington, a candidate put forward by the Ratepayers' Associa- tion. A well attended meeting of the Liberal com- mittee and workers of Bewsey ward was held in Wycliffe Hall on Saturday evening to promote the candidature of Councillor Shaw Green. The pro- ceedings were of a very enthusiastic character; and surprise was expressed that any opposition should be shown to ths candidate, who has daring his con- nection witk the Town Council rendered great ser- vices to the borough. Councillor Shaw Green gave a resupsi of the work of the past three years, allud- ing especially to the benefits to be derived from the yew Act, and to the increased work which would arise by the building of the new police premises, electric light station, hospitals, technical instruc- tion school, and the street improvements. A vote of confidence in him was unaaimausly passed. Mr. Linnasus Greening has been selected by the Conservatives of Orford Ward as the candidate in place of Councillor James Grace, retired. He will be opposed by Mr. Samuel Gough. a leading mem- ber .of the Ratepayers' Association.

There will also be a contest in Town Rail Ward where the retiring representative, Councillor Joseph Shillcock will be oppo=e I by Mr. James Cliff e of the Ratepayers' Association. The undermentioned have been returned unop- posed :

A STRAWBERRY PICNIC.

The Morning Leader, July 13;h, 1898, says :—"Messrs "S. Chivers St Sons, the well-known jam and jelly " manufacturers, understand the art of entertainment "no less tiaan the art of jam-making. On Tuesday they "invited a number of Pressmen to a strawberry picnic "at their farm at Iliston, Cambridge. The wondering " pickels were delighted with the sight of two-seore top- hatted visitors bending their backs to the willing task "of finding large strawoerries. It is just twenty-five " years since Messrs. Chivers started jam-making. " 'They now have a thousand acres of land under culti- vation, and employ, wheal busy, eight hundred hands. " 'The an factory is within the circle ot the fruit fields, " and the fruit can therefore behandled without delay•" It the luncheon which followed, a Cambridge ilier- man and J.P. said;—" When he was k h vv n over the " fact,)ry a year a:o he was struck by the healthy "appearance of the employes. the women alai girls " looking well and joyous, so different from the codi- tion of things in eime factories he had visited." A London manufacturer expr:sised the opinion that the root of Messrs. Chivers' success was the high excel- " lence of their goods, nhioh had made their mark in "the commercial world. People who had the goods " once, repeated their criers." The Official Reporter of the Royal Commission on Agriculture, 1895, says :—" Messrs. Claivers' factory at " Histon is now well-known throughout tat: country for " the excellence of its manufactures." Chivers' Gold Medal Jams and Jellies are sold by Grocers and Stores throughout the United Kingdom.

ST. JAMES' CHURCH.

The Rev. F. E. Powell, preached the first of a course of ,ermons on "Some Church Questions" on Sunday even- ing. The subject was " Did our Lord found a visible Church ?" and for his text t le rev. gentleman seleeted the words " Upon this rock I will build my Church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it." Se. Matt. xvi. 18. In the course of his remarks he stated that the Jewish Church consisted of the twelve tribes of Israel, so the Christian Church was built upon the foundation of the twelve Apostles, Christ Himself being tie bead corner-stone. He was constantly liken- ing His kingdom to visible things. That there was an invisible company drawn from alt sorts and conditions of religious opinions, even non-religious bodies, the Church had never denied. That invisible company, known to God alone, might contain, for all be knew, Turks, infidels, heretics, and even atheists. He ad- mitted that to the full, and no well-informed Church- man wouli care to deny it, but the so-called invisible Church was not mentioned or alluded to in the Scrip- tures from first to last ; but if there was any fact in history mere clearly establit bed than another it was that after the death el Jesus C'arist a new and visible society began to exist with visible rules, officers, rites, and ordinances in a visible way by holy baptism, con- firmation by laying on of hands, led together in a visible assembly to meet their invisible head. Without a visible church the precepts of Christ, and even the Passion of Christ, would soon have sunk into forgetfulness. THE REV E R TARBUCK AND THE WAR. _ _ _ _

The Vicar of St. Paul's (the Rev. E. R. Tarbuck) in the course at his serencia an Suuday evening made refer- ence to the war in the Transvaal. He remarked that other nations had admitted the fact that it was the rule and net the exception that Ragland did her best to develop the colony for the colony's sake, and those who had shown the natives the worst side of our civilization had been as a rule the riff-raft of ohr population or those who had for obvious reesens !Ailed to commend them- selves to their own country-men at borne and had con- aently betaken themselves and their vicious habits to the wider and less discipined regions of our colonies. But toe representatives of toe Government appointed to positions of responsitsility were chosen by those who had themselves been selected by the governing nation for their good qualities, aad at present on matters of mora- lity the heart of the English peeple wee fundamentally saund. It was quite natural that until the war was actually declared op the Baer Republic the political party in England represented just new by the Opposition members of Parliament shoulel do all they could to pre- vent our nation from comnittine any rash and nrecipi- ttte action, but as seen as President Kruger's ultitnetune was received, its terms were s) insulting, that our ranks were closed up and party feeling was laid aside. War was a terrible but sometimes a necessery evil, and in this case the country was at 009 in thinking that war became a necessity. Tney were net then to be consi- dered Jingoes when once war had begun because they cheered on their army and hop el for a speedy end. Our nation was never so united on any question as now. If they had seen certain members of Parliament making themselves an exception to this rule they felt that they did not represent their constituents and they were the ones who had aleenya proved themselves foreigners to the nationel sentiment. And the tele- gram from the seat of war read in the House of Com- mons a few days ago was significant ef this. The tele- gram stated that the troops concerned in the successful storming of the position held by the Boers on Dundee Hill were the King's Royal Rifles and the Dublin Fueiliers. Needless to say, because of their nationality, the name of the latter was reeeived by the members with indescribable enthusiasm.

WYCLIFFE BAND OF HOPE.

A very attractive programme was provided at the first public entertainment for this season in connection with Wycliffe Band of Hope, held on Monday evening. The Rev. John Yonge presided over a fairly large gathering. In the coarse of a few introductory observa- tions, the Chairman spoke of the pleasure it gave him to see so many young friends peesent on that occasion. It encored well for the success of the Baud of Hope. Mr. F. Merrell who was in charge of it was most anxious to make it a still greater success. Gee reason why it was called a band of hope was because boys and girls were the hope of the future. They were desirous that this bsloved country of ours should became a sober country and that the great vice and misery of drunkenness should be put an end to. They felt that if they could succeed in training up their boys and girls in the princi- ples of temperance they would be doing something to- wards making tSie people of the country, sober, happy. and prosperous. During the evening au excellent address was delivered •hy Mr. William Roberts (secretary of the Warriegton Temperance Federation). He spoke of his previous con- nection with the Wycliffe Band of Hope and the successful meetings held. Some people seemed to have got the idea that Band of Hope meetings were only for little children, but he thought it wee a great mistake. Some of their older children would be benefitted, and encouraged to keep the total abstinence pledge. He recmtly saw a young man whc had at one time attended Wycliffe sshool, under the influence of drink. Did not they think it was a disgrace to see either a young man or a young woman tumbling about their streets drunk ? He thouieht so,anl he thought parents cusht to encourage their children to keep the Bins! .st Hope pledge. He was afraid they did not do so. He had seen children belong- ing to their bands of hope being sent by their parents to public-houses for drink. It was not fair to the children. (Hear, hear.) It seas not setting a good example and was nulliying the efforts of those who were trying to bring them up in the right way. If parents would have their copper bier he would ask them to fetch it themselves and thus keep the temptation out of the way of the children. Some people thought it was right to take a small quantity but they might be bringing up someone who would not be content with a small quantity. Mr. Roberts proceeded to relate two typical instances which hsd come under his own observe- ton in Warrington showieg the terrible straits to which p,ople were brought by drink. In tionclusion he urged the children to be true to their pledge and spoke words of encouragement to the children. An excellent programme of trace] and instrumental music W3S given. An intereating feature was the per- formarce of a number of little girls in action songs, the first being a representation of sandwich men. Recita- tions were given by Miss Amy Shaw. " Found Dead Master Gerald Mason " Jerry Joy ;" Mr. F. Morrell, " Clerk Mugegins :" and Miss F. Bill, " Shoeblack Joe." Miss Lena Debson's fine c intralto voice was heard to advantage in the songs " Promise of • Life" and Daddr." For her faithful, able and feeling interpretation of the last named she was deservedly encored and complied with" The Star of Bethlehem." Mr. George Gough also gave a credit- able rendering of the song " The Soldier's Grave." " Where are you going to my pretty Maid," es as pleas- ingly interpreted by Mise K Whitfield and Master Harry Warburton, and the song " Buy my pretty flowers," was sung by Miss Lucy Evans. Mr F Ford highly pleased the audience by his performance on the cernet, and for his rendering ef " Queen of the Earth," he was eneored. At the close of a most enjoyable even— inff's entertainment the services of these who had taken Part in it were acknowledged on the proposition of Mrs.. F. Mcrral
