BEWSEY STREET, WARRINGTON.

THE campaign now waging British I,...tsses and their Causes. in Natal is the first instance in which smokeless powder has been used by two combatants, and, therefore, it is sure tofurnish us with some useful facts and lessons. The Boers are using a powder only a little inferior to our own, and hence the con- ditions may be regarded as being equal in this re- spect. When smokeless powder was first intro- duced there was a heated controversy amongst military men as to whether it would or would not increase the number of casualties in an engage- ment. Outsiders, using only their common sense declared that it was bound to do so; but military opinion was not very clear upon the point, except to the extent of admitting that it would give some advantages to a defending force. During the campaign for the recovery of Khartoum only the Anglo-Egyptian force used cordite cartridges, and little was to be learnt from the battles of Atbara and Khartoum, except that our fire did not obscure the fire zone, and enabled us to see the advancing Dervishes. In Natal we can now see some of the inevitable effects of the use of smokeless powder. It can hardly be denied that casualties have been increased on either side. Our own losses are considerable, and they are being unduly attributed to the excellent firing of the Beers and their mixed allies. Perhaps we may dismiss this point as in itself having only a small bearing upon the facts of the case. Good shooting is helped by smokeless powder,and the advantage has been equally distributed. We have undoubtedly had the advantage so far as artillery fire is concerned, and it has been more deadly in consequence. The Boer guns have been more easily silenced, and the losses of the Boers must have been correspondingly great, though we have little accurate knowledge about them. On the other hand, in our storming operations the smokeless powder has enabled the Boers to see and to hit our officers, who have been standing erect whilst their men were in cover. It seems clear that we must attribute our heavy casualties to twe things—the better aim a smoke- free air gave to the enemy,and the superiority thus given to men in a defensive position, who were barely visible to the storming force. In all our storming operations we shall notice that our casualties will be very large. But the use of cavalry is rendered easier and more deadly when the air is clear enough to enable the enemy's posi- tion and tactics to be discernible, and so one dis- advantage is almost, but not quite, balanced by another advantage. The effective use of cavalry or mounted riflemen has been marked in the two big battles already fought, and it will probably continue to the end of the war, and increase with the numbers of the enemy actually in the field. The Boers on foot can only meet cavalry by a few dropping shots, and then they must reach their horses and fly. They are only able to attack infan- try, when they themselves are under cover, by rifle or Maxim-gun tire, and when the infantry are near enough to come on at the charge with the bayonet they must stampede. We think we have given some reason for our heavy British losses.

The men of affairs who conduct the War Office are not disposed, apparently, to turn to account at once the principal lesson of the battles in Natal. Regimental officers for the front have been ordered to assume distinctive badges—bright stars on the helmet, sashes, white facings for regimentals, and the like—and they are obeying with. more alacrity than common sense. We should have thought that distinctive signs for officers were intended for the convenience of the rank and file, not as an advertisement for the enemy. The stars and white facings which have been ordered for some of the officers will shew up in strong relief on the veld. It is right and proper that officers should lead the way. and should not seek cover. This is part of

===== 0002601_18991027_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
the duty. But according to the military authorities they must, in addition, say " Here am I" to the enemy ; and there is neither sense nor usefulness in it. It is quite possible thac in future wars dis- tinctive dress of all kinds will nave to be cast aside. The Gordons suffered so severely in the second Natal battle solely because their kilts made them so conspicuous. It was suggested many months ago that Highland regimeuts should be deprived of their kilts, and a great outcry arose against eliminating a picturesque and historic gatb from the army. But we cannot always sacrifice good men on the altar of history and picturesqueness.

THE unconscious transfer- The EvPil Genius in ence to one region of thought olitics. and belief of views and theories which have lost vitality in some other province, is one of the puzzles of history. For ex- ample the gambler is invariably as superstitious as the untutored savage, and he believes in omens and tokens. It was inevitable that in politics there should be traces of the same habit of mind. Few persons any longer believe in the control of natural and physical phenomena by malignant agencies or personalities, but there is quite a dis- position to think that domestic and internal politics are subject to the periodical influence of some evil genius. At the present moment there are many people who see in the events of South Africa the effects of an evil genius at home. We need not name the statesman in question. A good deal of argument may be adduced in support of the theory. At times it seems to be very convinc- ing, and at others it is weaker. Men say that but for the evil genius there would have been no real trouble and no war. Such a theory implies the possession and use of influ- ences which are almost incompatible with representative institutions, enlightened public opinion, a free Ptess, and Cabinet govern- ment. People have to be convinced,and not bribed; they have to be reasoned with, not coerced ; and sane and responsible politicians have to be brought into line. An evil spirit, working by sub- tle and secret means, is no longer possible in this country, because it presupposes ignorance, super- stition, and inability to follow a long controversy intelligently. We must allow for the free play of common sense in British affairs. A man may blunder into serious difficulty, but he cannot play the part of a vicious supernatural being. He can- not withhold, distort, or falsify information. We wish it were possible to say that nothing in the character of the evil genius had been at work else- where. Dr. St7eyn, the President of the Orange Free State, who has issued a Proclamation annex- ing parts of Cape Colony to the country he governs seems to have been an evil counsellor to President KRUGER, and to have suggested to him the terms of the ultimatum in which he forfeited all title to our regard as a sensible diplomatist, with theories he need not have put to the test of military violence. Dr. LEYDS has been another evil genius. He has distorted the information he has forwarded to Pretoria. He has buoyed up the Transvaal Boers with hopes of European intervention. He has been buying arms and implements of war to prepare the way for that combination between the two Boor Republics which is the ugliest fact in the whole situation. We have no intention of includ- ing President KRUGER in the category of the evil genius. He has undoubtedly trusted a little too much in his own cleverness, and believed tos much in the distorting effects of party feeling in this country. He has yielded tp the ambitious designs of President STEYN, now avowed by the paper annexation of the Kimberley region and all the British territory belonging to Cape Colony north of the Vaal river. But we believe that he has been honestly striving to act according to his lights, and that his hand has been forced by others, some of whom have paid for their temerity on the battlefield. Dr. Leyds and Dr. Steyn have played on the ignorance of the Boers, and manufactured a situation which has led to war, and war out of which no good can come to Boer rule. They have been evil genii of the bad type, and the mischief they have wrought is only too apparent. The Boers are being daily undeceived. They will ultimately find out that they have also been be- trayed.

There is nothing of a particularly congratula- tory nature about the fact that Warrington is in a fair way towards possessing one of the finest police stations in the country. Had it been a technical institute, a free library, or something of that kind, the inhabitants might be pardoned a feeling of pride in a building which is to cost at least £22,000. They could then, with unctuous satisfaction, have bid the stranger pause and admire the handsome structure erected by the "city fathers" for the benefit of the city's sons. But,an architectural ornament to the town though it will be, the police station is hardly a subject upon which even a guide of the eloquent type encountered by Mark Twain could dilate at any length with results at all satisfactory. His hearers might be impressed—even as the humorist was— but they would probably take such a distorted view of matters as would bring the guide's grey hairs in sorrow to the grave. An efficient police force and an adequate police station are necessities of the age, however, and it behoves the" city fathers" to pay strict attention to the provision and maintenance of both. Warrington has long been sadly behindhand in the matter of accommo- dation for the seat of justice, but the defect is to be thoroughly remedied and on Tuesday the Mayor formally laid the foundation stone of the spacious block of buildings which has been designed to meet the wants of the town and the demands of the Government in this respect.

revival in an American law journal of the dis- cussion of the case of Mrs. Maybrick has brought out some interesting facts as to the rights of women who marry foreigners. Our own Act of Parliament of 1870 makes simple the position of English maidens who choose husbands belonging to another country. A married woman is deemed to be the subject of the State of which her husband is for the time being the subject. She need make no declaration. Her individuality as an English- woman is sacrificed at the altar where she promises to love, honour and obey—or it may be at the registry, for either contract is held by our law to make her subordinate to the man. The American girl, however, does not lose her birthright in this way. Once an American she is always an American, except she makes specific renunciation. The English- woman on the death of her husband may claim re-naturalisation; but, husband or no husband, the American lady is recognised as a citizen of the United States. The people across the Atlantic are proud of the fact that they do not expatriate their daughters because they have chosen their life companions from another race. The senti- ment sounds very noble, but in practice it does not work very well. What every woman, as well as every man, should be able to claim is the full rights of citizenship of the country in which either i may happen to reside. It s better, if dwelling in Great Britain, to have the full benefits of English laws than to no able to make a futile appeal, as Mrs. Maybrick has done,to the American Embassy. An American woman who had resided twenty years in England, and had complaints to make regarding her treatment, for example, when travel- ling in the Far East, would hardly excite mach enthusiasm enthusiasm for her cause among the Americans ;

and, according to their law, she would have no ground for asking the officials at our Foreign Office to take action on her behalf. She would be far better situated if, by virtue of being the wife of a British citizen, she could demand the aid of the British Government, as a British subject.

Those who have been clamouring for war will be the first to protest against the advance in the price of bread which has followed upon milit try operations. At the corn exchange in Warrington, on Wednesday, there was a substantial rise in the price of wheat, and it is only a natural sequence that we should hear there has been an advance in the price of flour. The Master Bakers' Association has now decidell to increase the quartern loaf by one half-penny. Small as the advance is, it will be felt in many homes of the poor. The far reach- ing effect of war is thus experienced in places where the pinch of poverty is most acutely felt and it would be well if fomentors of war would think a little more of these things in the future.

We thought it would be strange if some of the irresponsible members of the Conservative party did not " have a slap" at Alderman Roberts and his forthcoming election to the Mayoralty. It turns out that we were right in our conjecture for at a meeting of the Council of tho Warrington Conservative Association last week a few mem- bers contrived to get rid of some of their spleen. One gentleman credited Alderman Roberts with an intention to murder—what ?—why, nothing more valuable than" every virtue of Conservative principles." We fear that the Alderman would have a greater difficulty in discovering the said virtues than in murdering them afterward*. Another individual believed "that the dignity of the town could not be safely entrusted in the hands of Alderman Roberts," and went on to accuse the Mayor-elect of being ad- dicted to the use of language more suited to Billingsgate than polite society. This gentleman need not fear for the dignity of the town any more than his brother speaker need be alarmed about the safety of the " virtues of Conservative principles." Experience has shown that the dignity of the town is not easily lowered, and if Alderman Roberts cannot uphold it as ably as some of his predecessors have done,well ! we are sorry for the dignity. Really, these remarks emanating from obscure and irresponsible units of Conservatism were not worth noticing, but for the fact that they do possess the small merit of being funny.

RUNCORN COUNTY COURT.

INJURIES TO I MARE AT FRODSHAM. At the Runcora County Court, on Tuesday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffeulkee and a jury, an interest- ing case was heard in which James Andrews, farmer. of Mickledale, Frodsham, sued Alexaader Bate, farrier, also of Frodsham, for £l6 18s. 61. in respect to injuries caused to a mare of his by the defendant's hors—Mr. Pugh, barrister, instructed by Messrs. Linaker and Linaker, solicitors, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Greer, barrister, instructed by dr. Joseph Burton, solicitor, appeared for the defendant. In opening the case, Mr. Pugh stated the facts to be, that Mr. Andrews was a farmer at Frodsham, and Mr. Bate was a farrier of the same place. Complainant ha put a pony mare in his field, which he had purchased some twelve months previously. On the 4th of Septem- ber he went to the Marsh for the purpose of getting the pony to do some work and then found that a chestnut stallion belonging to the defendant was there and subse- quent examination of the mare disclosed the fact that it had been seriously injured by the horse,and it was upon the extent of those damages that the claim was based. James Andrews, of Mickledale Farm, Froisham, bore out the barrister's statement. He found the pony to be suffering from kicks on the near heck ; the near shin bone ; both her heels were trodden and crushed, and she was kicked on the off hind shin and the right elbew. He had been unable to work the pony since. On the sth of September he wrote to Mr. Rate about the pony and on the following day whilst he was at a blacksmiths in Overton getting a horse shod, he met Mr. Bate and had a conversation with him. Mr. Bate said whatever damage had been done ke would pay for. Witness valued the pony be- fore the accident at £l2 and had an offer for its pur- chase of £lO, He did not thank she would fete", £5 if sold at present, and he entertained no hope of her ' recovery.—Cross-examined by Mr. Greer : He did not think it was unreasonable to spend all the money he did upon the pony, he felt it was his duty to try and doctor it and get it better. John William Bate, veterinary surgeon. M.R.O Y.S., said he was calied in to see the plaintiff's pony. He had the pony taken out and found a lacerated wound about 1i inches on the left off shoulder. Three wounds on the off hind shank bone; two wounds on the near shank bone of the hind leg; a lacerated wound on the near off; and the heels of the near hind leg crushed off ; also, the heels of the off hind leg were bruised, and the animal was stiff in both hind lees and the off fore log. Ile attended the hrse up to October 4th and had been paid his bill £4 2e. 6d- John Davies. farmer, Aston, said he had seen the pony on tha Bth of September. At the time he saw the pony he valued it at £5. If there had been no wsunds, and it had been safe and quiet in harness it would be worth £lO. Harry Hutchinson, farmsr,of Frolshans. said he knew the grey mare belonging to Andrews. He should say, it was worth £lO before the accident for the work it was doing. After the accident it would be depreciated by one half its value. Joseph Ellison, Frodsham, a mineral water manufac- turer, said the pony had deteriorated in value through the accident £5 or £6. Richard Oultrarn, horse and cattle dealer, Frodsham, said on the 4th of July he offered Andrews £9 for the horse, but he refused, and would take no less than tl2. Wilfred Shallcross, cattle dealer, said he too offered £8 1.0 for the animal on the 26th of April. Frederick Houghton, blacksmith, testified to over- hearing the c,onvfirsation between defendant and plaintiff. He heard Mr. Bate say he would pay for whatever damage the mare had sustained. This ended the ease for plaintiff. Alexander Bate,of Frodsham,the defendast,said he was a farrier, and it would be impossible for the horse to get across from his field into the complainant's. He did not hear of the pony being in the field until his boy came back and told him about it. He asked Andrews whether much ailed the pony, and he said there were a few scratches on the leg. Witness also offered him at a subsequent interview a bottle of lotion, and said he could have the choice of two ponies until his own got better. Witness went down to Andrews place and saw the pony. It was in poor condition, and one could count all its ribs. He examined it and there was not a cut that could be seen. There were bruises or scratches on the animal in the positions as the plaintiff had stated, but not one of them had briren the skin. He did not agree with the mode of treatment and had offered Andrews to take the animal and doctor it ; lend him one of his ponies while he was doing so; and also he had asked that the animal he sold to him. Andrew; would do neither, but said he had "got him under finger and thumb and as he (Bate) was fond of law he should have plenty." Rate's further evidence was a direct contra- diction of the plaintiff's resneeting the conversations. The horse was worth abeu: £4 or £5 and at the outside £6 would be a capital price for any Iceland or foreign peny. He was of the opinion that a half-sovereign woald have covered the injaries.—Cross-examined by Mr. Pugh: His wire did suggest that the matter be re- ferred to Mr. Yarwood for arbitration, but the matter fell through because Andrews wanted the Veterinary Surgeon in the arbitration. Robert Clowes Edwards, M.R.C.V.S., of 5, Newgate- street, Chester, said he went in company with the de- fendant and examined the mare in the presence of plain- tiff. The general condition of the /mimel was poor, and he would say that it was so apart from the fact that it was under treatment- There were kicks and bruises upon the animal as far as he could remember, but noth- ing serious, lie was of the opinion that the animal should have been fit for work in a fortnight. It was simply a matter for a little lotion and a little patience. Francis William Cosgra.ve, M.R.C.V.S., practising at Neston, stated he examined the mare on the 23rd of October, and found nothing whatever the matter with it. It was worth about £7, which was a capital price. George Yarwoed, farmer, Sutton Weaver. sid he was the Yarwood esiled upon to arbitrate, and considered at that time—the 20:h of September, that £3 would be ample damages. Counsel having addressed the jury His Honour summed up. The jury found for the plaintiff.after a brief retirement for £lO. His Honour entered judgment accordingly.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, OCTOBER 27, 1899

RUNCORN NOTES,

[By A CORRESPOIIDENT.I As anticipated the powers that be upon the Run- corn School Bard adhered to established precedent, and in succession to the Rev. H. B. Gore (resigned) co-opted his successor to the St. Edward's Mission —the Rev Joseph Chambers—to the vacancy. The matter was virtually agreed upon by the whole Board, yet it is interesting to note that Mr. F. J. Norman was not present. This might be a gentle protest, for I know he has an objection to clericals of any kind acting outside the cloth.

Farther interesting news is that affecting the salt industry of Cheshire, Worcestershire, Stafford- shire, and Lancashire. I understand an important meeting of the Investigation Committee appointed by the salt manufacturers ef the Kingdom to formu- late a combination scheme was held at Northwich on Wednesday. The committee appointed as chair- man Mr. Thomas Wood, managing director of the Salt Union, and as vice-chairman Mr. Howitt, managing director of Messrs. Bowman, Thompson, and Company. The committee, lam informed, practically accomplished the formation of the com- bination, which is intended to receive the stability of prices and promote the interests of the trade generally, and that the terms of the amalgamation were almost agreed upon.—No doabt,the result will be beneficial all round, but it is certain that its advent will be welcomed in Runcorn and especially at Weston Point where the Salt Union Works are situate.

The recent stack tire at Halton has given us a very striking illustration of the inadequacy of the Runcorn Fire Brigade appliances to cope with a fire of any serious dimensions, in a place where there is no assistance to be olatained from a gravi- tated water supply, and we should profit by the illustration, by as early as opportune replacing the somewhat out-of-date"manuarby a more up-to-date "steamer." The Fire Brigade would then be bettor equipped for their work and much more fitted to contest a conflagration should one break 0-it in the higher portion of the town; especially in that part where the pressure in the mains is weakest.

There is a markel revival in the chemical trade in Mid-Cheshire, and the demands from markets in all parts of the world are reported to be greater than for twelve months past Upwards of seventy fresh hands have been taken on by Messrs. Brunner, Mond and Co., at their Winningtoa (Northwich) works alone. Runcorn and Widnes also show signs of a marked improvement, and the indications all round point to an exceedingly busy winter.

WIDNES MUNICIPAL ELIICTIONS.

The municipal elections have passed over very quietly in Widnes. There was one vacancy to be filled in each of the six wards. It avaa not expected that there would be contests in five of the wards, but as a new candidate had come forward in Victoria Ward, in the person of Mr. Thomas Atherton, coal merchant, Lacey-street, a fight was anticipated there, but when the nominations closed in the afternoon it was found that the retiring member, Councillor C Flynn, had vacated the field. The retiring members fir the other wards were all re-elected without opposition, the full list of candidates being :—Farn worth Ward, Councillor T Shuttleworth ; Flatten Ward, Councillor J Midwood ; Simms' Cross Ward,Councillor Owens; Victoria Ward, Mr Thomas Atherton ; Waterloo Ward, Councillor T Carney ; and West Bank Ward,Couacillor H A Richards.

AN AMERICAN IN TROUBLE AT WIDNES.

At Widnes, on Monday, Edward Kyle, a Chicago man, was charged with being on the premises of Horatio Syred, pawnbroker, Waterloo-road, at 1.30 a.m. on Saturday. Cyrus Syred, prosecutor's sun, said he was asleep in the back bedroom. About half-pait one he heard footsteps. Witness listeued for some time, and he heard footfalls in the upper rooms. He raised the alarm, and Police-constable Turner, who was at hand, searched the premit.s with witness, but could not find anyone inside. On looking about the outside of the premises they found prisoner behind the signboard in a crouch- ine position. He was asked to come down and be came. —Mr. Syred said that in his opinion the man had got down the chimney. This, however, proved to be not correct, as there was no indication of anything being disturbed. On a further search being made,a small bar of iron was found.—Prisoner, in defence, pleaded being drunk, and that he was in search of a place to sleep. He was a seaman, and had spent all his money carelessly in Liverpool. The Bench as a warning to him as to his behaviour and as a protection to the public, sent him to prison for two months' hard labour. THREE MEN INJURED BY A RUNAWAY HORSE.

Shoqly after noon on Monday last, Mr. William Dun- bavin, farmer, Old Hall, Weston, was driving a cart load of vegetables from some land by the new works, in c-mpanv with his brother John. They had a young horse wlfch they had only just started to break-in in the leading chains, and a steadier one in the shafte. They stopped to rest awhile before ascending the hill in Banke's-lane, and whilst there a " pot-cart "from Run- corn pa,.siag at a trot Caused the young animal to bolt like a mad thing. The result was it threw itself down and caused the other horse to fall on top of it. Mr. William Dunba,vin was thrown by the horse, and his brother John, describing the affair, stated he hardly knew where he get to. Both, however, sustained severe injuries. William Dunbavin having his collar-bone on the right side fractured, and his brother having his right shoulder dislocated. An old man named William Pownall, a boiler-maker at the new works, who was on the way to his dinner went to the assistance of the Dunbavine, but he too was injured by the struggling horse, and sustained a fractured rib and collar bone. Yoang Mr. Bankes, of Weston, on becoming acquainted of the accident, at once rode to Runcorn for medical assistance and Drs. McLennan and Robinson were en the scone of the disaster, in about half an hour after its occurrence. The injured men are now all progressing as well as can be expected.

ALARMING STACK FIR.

The Runcorn fire briga.ds was summoned to an out- break of fire, on the farm of M. Alfred Grice, Halton Lldge, about five o'clock on Tuesday. On arriving there it was found that an untbrashed stack of wheat, of about £2OO value, was on fire in the centre of about twenty other stacks of farm produce. The brigade which was accomeanied by Police-Superintendent Pearsoa, and Superintendent Wright, proved inadequate to cope with the flames, and beta Superintend-ate returned to Run- corn, and telephoned for the Wilderspool steam fire. engine (Warrington). This arrived on the scene shortly after eight, but even then the efforts of b3th brigades had to be c3nfiaed to preventing the spread of the fire, Operations were kept up all the night through, and it was not until 7 o'clock the f3llowing morning that the Wilderepool Brigade were able to leave. The Runcorn Brigade returned at about 11. o'clock, at which time the fire was spent, and everything rendered safe.

RUNCORN EVENING SCTIOOLS.

PRIZE DISIRIBUTION.

The prizes were distributed in connection with the Evening Continuation Classes, at the Victoria-road Schools on Monday evening last, by the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood (cairrnen of the Runcorn School Board) who was supported by the Rev. Joseph Onarnbers, Mr. William Handles', Mr. A. Lightbarn (assistant clerk to the School Board), Mr. J. F. Moulton (head master),Miss Kilburn (bead mistress),and other members ef the teach- ing staff. Mr. Mculton, the head master, presented the annual report. During the last session of the evening schools the numbers on the rolls were :-80 girls' and 90 boys. Tee average attendance for the year of those qualified to earn grants was—boys' department 46, girls' depart- ment 44. The full grant in both departments was awarded by the inspe tor for each subject, the total grant being—boys, £56 12s 61; girls £4O 103 ; total £97 2e 6d. Owing to what was known as the 17s 6d iinel in the Evening Schools Cede, the full grant was not paid by the Department. There was a reduction under article 17a of £lB 7s 6d Toe Inspector (Mr. Ballance), who visited the school five or six times during the session,reported as f ellows : —Boys' Department :—" This is a well taught scheel." Girls' Department : " Tnis school continues to do well. The lessons given in science of common things are par- ticularly good." Thera was no annual examination by the inspostor. Examinations were held unier the aus- pices of the Union of the Lancashire and Cheshire Insti- tutes, and during the last ses,ion 118 pupils presented themselves for examination. In the result 15 were awarded first Class certificates, 43 second class and 48 third class. He thought in an examination of that kind tho pupils who had the courage te compete for the cer- tificates had done very creditably. (Applause.) The Rev. Canon Wood said it was a very pleasant thing to corase there and distribute the prizss that night as requested by the Board at their last meeting. He had a great opinion of the value of seek continuation classes in the work of education genezelly. These night schools had a special function to perform in the work of education. They were intermediate between the elementary school and the technicel school. Ho would like everybody to remember that these particular night classes were intended to carry on education further tkan is esuld be carried on in the ordinary day school. A. boy who left the day school at 13, or a girl who left it perhaps earlier, could not he considered to be thoroughly educated. He (the vicar) stayed at school lentil he was In years of age ; and he did not feel that he was at all thoroughly educated yet. He had been learning all his life, and he hoped that the boys and girls present would never consider their education cemolete at 13. It was only jest beginnine, as a matter of fact. These classes ought to be a sort of stepping stone from the one stage to the other, and in that way they held a very important position in the general scheme of the education of the people of the country. Refer- ring to the class for men recently established, the Vicar said Mr. Moulton's mind Was very much set upon the success of that particular class, and he was very glad to see that he had got a certain number together, and that they were really settling down and learning in earnest. He was pleased to hear that the class had in- creased in numbers very mush, and he was particularly glad to hear that one of his (the vicar's) own men was amongst them. He thought these who received prizes would understand that they were given first of all in recoenHon of their own particular efforts, and se- condly because those who provided the prizes wi4hed to express their interest in the work that was being so well carried on by Mr. Mouton, Miss Kilbure, and the staff. W ESTON.

PARISIIE COUNCIL—A meeting of the Parish Counci was held in the Weston Point School on Friday even- ing, the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood in the chair. There were also present : Messrs Allen, Savage, Garner, Griffiths, and James Collier (clerk).—The Clerk read a letter he had received from Mr. C. L. Evans, agent to Major Orred, in reply to a communication (as autho- rised by the Council) calling Mr. Evans's attention to the dangerous state of the railings round the Common Pit. Mr. Evans replied thus : "If your Council are of opinion that it i 3 important to fence the pit round I am willing t,) fence Maj)r Orred's portion if the Runcorn Commissioners will fence their side. It is no use to do one without the other."—After some discussion it was decided that the clerk be requested to reply to the effect that in the judgment of the Council there is a wide difference between allowing a fence to decay which pro- tects vehicles on the high road, and asking authority to fence a pond from common-land.

THE NEW REGULATIONS WITH REFERENCE TO DAIRIES

AND COWSHEDS.

Mr. Mclntyre said the Committee conaidered these regulations about six weeks ago, and they had nothing to do now but to ask the Council to adopt them. He moved that the regulations be adopted by the Council. Mr. Whitlow : What abont 800 cubic feat air space.? Mr. Mclntyre : That des not apply to plople w:aose cows go out. It is confined to shippons in Urban dis- trict's. Mr. Wend ward said they applied to the Lena! Govern- ment Board to allow them to have 450 cubic feet allowed as air space for cattle in thwir district, and they distinctly rmfusecl to grant the the conceision. He thought 800 cubic feet was out of all proportion to the cattle. _ _ . _

Mr. Linak,r suggested that the 800 feet of air space might be reduced to 600. Mr. Wooiward said there would be a diffieulty in obtaining the consent of the Local Government Board to any reduction. Mr. Whitlow thought they ought to have the shippons inspected to see if they were well lighted and ventilated. Mr. Woodward seconded the adoption of the regu- lations, and suggested that a recommendation be added to them to the effect that in the case of new buildings air space of 450 cubic feet be allowed. Mr. Antwiss thought a great improvement might be made in the cattle troughs. He thought the disease might be attributed to the accumulation of filth round the troughs. He pronosid thit a recommendation to that effect be inserted in the regulations. Mr. Whitlow seconded. Eventually thi.s was substi- tuted for an amendment that 450 cubic feet of air space should be required by the regulations. On a vote being taken it was found that there were seven for the amendment and seven against. The amend- ment was supp2rted by the Chairman's casting vote and carried.

Admiral Jones-Parry moved a further amendment that the whole question be deferred for a fortnight: Mr. Linaker seconded, and the proposition was car- ried.

SHOCKING CRUELTY TO I WlFE.—Levi Bradshaw, 156, Mercer-street, Newton, was charged with persistent cruelty to his wife Maria Bradshaw on the dates previous to the 30th September. whereby he caused her to leave him —Mr. Smith, solicitor. of Wigan. appeared for the complainant. He said the parties were married on the 19th of June. 1894, at the registry office, Wigan. Almost from the outset the man had been in the habit of assaulting and abusing his wife. Bight months after they were married she left him on account of his assaulting her, and defendant subsequently appeared before the magistrates. It ;had culminated in very shocking treatment which his witnesses would detail. Two months ago the woman was in bed. There were fortunately- no children of the marriage, but the woman had taken in a little baby ta nurse. She was in bed with the baby when he Came and stood over her with a paraffin lamp and threatened to throw it on her. She appealed to him not to do it because of the baby, and induced him to go away. She was so frightened that she got out of ked and sat downstairs all night. On two occasions she was compelled to run out of the house and on one occasion be asked her if she knew how hen's necks were screwed off when they were killed. He said that if she would turn the lamp down be would show her how it was done. He turned the lamp dowft and male a rush at her. She ran out of the house and got a constable to go to him. He was continually drunk and she had to work practically the whole time to keep them b3th. —Several witnesses gave evidence in support of this state- ment. —Defendant on oath, said he had been in a state of bad health for two years and his wife would not ceok him meat to " work on." When he had asked her for anything she had cursed and sworn " something cruel." He was not able to work now because of her treatment. Ile only had drink at the week ends.—The Magistrates retired to consider their decision and on their return the Chairman said they had given the case careful consideration and had decided to make a separation order the husband to make an allowance of 58. a week towards his wife's maintenance.

He was obliged to take him into custody ; when he did so Brown cause behind him, and struck him on on the back of the ear, and knocked him to the ground. Mr. Chorley, Buttermarket-street, came to his assist- ance and held Brown till P.S. Cheoney came on the scene. They took the men to the Police Station. His (witness's) elbow was hurt in the fall—Mr. Cherley.and Joseph Cooper, a 'bus conductor, gave corroborative evidence. The Chief Constable said both the prisoners had been before the court once before for drunkenness. He wonld like to take that opportunity of thanking Mr. Charley for the aseistance he had rendered to the police, and also several witnesses who came forward voluntarily to give evidence.—Mr. Birchall, said it was very henour- able of them.—The magistrates decided to fine Brewn 53. and costs, for drunkenness,aret 103. and costs or 14 days imprisonment forlthe aseault and fined Henriesey 53. and costs, Alderman Sutton remarking that Brown had got off well, as it was a serious matter to assault a police- man.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, OCTOBER 27, 1899.

THE WHITE SPECTRE.

One in every eleven of the population of Great Britain dies of consumption. Nearly half those who reach middle ago suffer, or have suffered, from some variety of it. No less than a quarter of a million (250,000) are dying of it at the present moment. Consumption is not inherited. but contracted, the germs attacking chiefly the weak and undernourished. In true consumption the lungs and other tissues are destroyeii, including the bones. No remedy for it has yet been found. There is therefore plenty of reason for the fear people feel of the White Spectre of the North. Did Mrs. Dinah Keep have consumption P Six medi- cal men said she had. And they believed they were right. Still she disappointed them all by getting well. She lost her good health in January, 1878. She had been confined, and after that event failed to regain her na'ural strength. Food—the only source of strength— had no attractions for her ; she didn't want it. Of emirs° she ate a mouthful now and then ; else she had starved to death. But those bits of food set going such a palpitation of the heart, and such pains in the chest, she wished she had let them alone. It was a miserable dilemma.

" Often," said Mrs. Keep, Moly, " I was seized with windy spasms, and for hours the pain was agolising. At night I was so bad sometimes that I used to walk the room till nearly morning. Even at best I got less rest than I needed.

" After a time I began to have a cough, a hollow- sounding cough such as yen bear in consumptives. And then the night sweats ! My linen was soaked with them. All this looked like consumption, and I knew what the end of that must b 3. I wasted and lost flesh until I hardly knew myself. My clothes no longer fitted me —tily merely hung on me.

" And the weakness that went with it! I never fan- cied before what the word weakness meant. I could neisher wash nor dress myself. There was no more tripping up and downstairs for me; the people picked me up and carried me. Fortunately for my friends was a light burden. All day long I lay back in an easy chair and watched others go about. Alv own working days were gone for over. At least, so I thought. " I shau!d soon have to leave this bright world, and se no more of my little children who were sa dear to me. But Go i's will be done.

" Often during the night a feeling of suffocation came over me, and I gasped and struggled fer breath. Som. timei I did not expect to live till morning.' " No less than six doctors at different periods at- tended me ; and all of them, one after another, finally give me up. They said I bad consumption, and must die of it sooner or later. And yet I went on for ten year in this way, now better, new worse.

" Many a time my husband was sure I was dying,and on several occasions the prayers of the Church were offered up for me. "In 1888, at 3 tinal when I app,ared to be at death's door, nay h asband read in a small book about cases as had as mine having been cured by Mother Seige l's Syrup. It looked as if it were impossible, but to gratify him I began taking the medicine, and never looked back aft,r that thy. In a few weeks I got back my appetite, and my food agreed with me. Gradually my strength returned, and in six nionths I toes in perfect health. Sine) then I have never kuown any illness, and that is years ago new, showing how complete the cure was. That I am indebted to Mother Seigel's Syrup for my life' and health there is no doubt, and you may publish what I say as widely as you choose."— (Signsd) (Mrs.) DINAH KEEP, Chalgrove Field House, 12, Magdalen•road, Cowley, Oxford, August 3, 1899. Mrs. Keep's trouble was bad enough, but it was not co,sumplon ; it was dyspepsia, with wasting—an imitation of consumption which constantly deceives the best doctors, as this instance proves. Prevalent as consumption is, there is a host of chronic dyspeptics who have the appearance and many of the symptoms of lung disease', but who would recover if they were treated for the digestive disorder, for which the surest remedy is Mother Seigel's Syrup.

" MOUNTS AND RIDES OFF."

THE CYCLE BOOM OVER.

At 98, Radnor-street, Hulme, Manchester, is the cycle establishment of Mr. Thomas Hughes, whom a " Sauth Manchestlr Chronicle " commissioner lately visited. The " boom " in cycles is over, but (as the newspaper RI an found) the trade ail' had its sensatiens, as Mr. Hughes' story showed. " Yes,cycle making and cycle riding are good things," said Mr. Hughes. "About twelve months ago I was in a terrible state of collapse. I had no enPrgy or life in me at all, and could do no work. My appetite became very bad; in fact, I could not eat any of the meals set before me, and a most extraordinary thing, I was losing my memory. All I wanted to do was to sit down. One time I went out and could not find my way home • again, I was so bad. I could no lcmger mount and ride ' off as of old." _ - .

"But did you not take any medicine ?" queried the pressman. .• Yes. In conversation with a chemist's assistant I told my tale of woe, and he, without hesitation, recom- mended me to try Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. I took his advice. In a week after first commencing my appetite began to return, and now," added Mr. Hughes with a hearty laugh, " 1 can eat three as good meals a day as any man in England, and enjoy them. I con- tinued taking Dr. Williams' pink pills, and can now do a hard day's work; my memory has returned and my eyesight is completely restored ; in lac', I am 113 VI com- pletely cured, but I still take the pills as a precaution. The precaution is a wise one. A serious illness leaves serious effects, and while there is no object in taking medicine of any sort needlessly, it is always advisable to continue for a few days after the last symptoms have disappeared, so as to avoid a relapse. Dr. Williams' pink plus have pernaoently cured paralysis,rheumatism sciatica, neuralgia, the after-effects of infl ienza, con- sumption, bronchitis, anx,nia, and all diseases arising from impoverished blood ; but it is the genuine pills alone which cure—no one was ever cured by a sub- stitute such as some shopkeepers offer. The genuine pills may be obtained:post free for two and ninepence from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company,Halborn Viaduct, London, and it is best to send direct for them when sub- stitutes are pushed.

THE NORTHERN UNION. LANCASHIRE SENIOR COMPETITION.

Warrington's Poor Display at Salford. The first meeting between Werriagtoa and Salford this season, took place at New Barns on Saturday in the presence of about 9,000 spectators. Werriagtett uniertook the jeurney with every confidence of success for their oppoeents were short of almost half the regular fifteen from the exigencies of C:unty feotball, and iisjuriee sustained in play. The visitors took a full team and were accernpenied by a couple of hlndrscl sup- porters. Weather of the pleasantest description again favoured the footballers, and this had much to do with the size of the crowd which wended its way to New Barns. Toe fi.ld Was in excellent order, but soon after the start the light became very bad, owing to a loca' mist which overhung the enclosure. Mr. J. Underwood, Wigan, was referee,end the teams faced each other,com- posed as follows : Warrineten : Ashurst, ftel back ; Fish, J me;, Isher- wood, and Harris, threequarters ; Bate and Dric.k worth, halt backs ; Taylor, Naylor, Merry weather, Fairhutst, Boardman, Sudlow,Seholtze.aad Eden. forwards. Salfcrd : D Smith, full back ; Norris, Crocker, Thomas, and Pesrson, threqearters ; Doran (captain) anti Phillips, half backs ; Heath, Shaw, Howley, Shore, Haworth, Llewellyn, Jones, and Fisher, forwards. Jones started the game for Warrington,Phillips making a return which caused Ashurst to be tested. He saved finely before the Salford forwards and punted back with interest. Werrington got well down and took up the attack in promising style. Early on, r tyl)r broke away at the quarter line and pat in a drop kick which nearly ended in a try as Smith only spout-el in the nick of time. The ball however, left the toe of Taylor in a line with the goal and to everybody's surprise it passed over the bar and Warringtea were credited with a lucky two points. Salford were not long in equalising however, for Isherwood making a peor kick gave Phomaa an e lay mark in front of goal. Phillips had no difficulty in placing a goal. Oa even terms once more the t .anus played hard for supremacy. Warrington raced down to the horn,' line where Bate twice gave passes to Fish when the latter had go cleance of making headway. Good punting by Isherwood kept Salfoid on the defence. Jones received from Bate when close in but was a bit slow in getting off, and Smith grassed him near the line. Warrington lost possession in the pack arid N)rris taking the ball from Doran got clear and ran from his own line to within ten yards of the Warrington line, where he was overhauled by Isherwood and knockei in touch. It was a good eff nt and Warrington were lucky to escape. Warrington soon ran the ball hack again but made poor use of opportunities near the line. Bate in particular seas very faulty in passing to the three- quarters. Faulty play resulted in Phillips and Smiths clearing nicely, and the home forwards put in a grand dribble which brought about Warrington's downfall. Fairhurst ran back and impeded Ashurst, with the result that neither collared the ball, and Bewley got a soft try near the posts. Phillips again added the goal. This reverse was entirely due to Warrington's loose play when attaeltiag. Salford played up strongly after this, and gave Warrington a lot of trouble on the defence. Fish and Harris both made progress on the wings but were well wat,thed, while Merryweather was cautioned for a needless tackle of Dan Smith. The Warringten backs mulled repeatedly in catching the ball, and Ashuret had hard work to save on many occasions. The interval came with Salford leading by 7 points to 2. Death re-started hostilities and Warrington being early penalised Salford attacked strongly. Pearson made two brilliant attempts to score,Ashurst and Harris checking his speedy career in the nick of time. Scrim- mages were fought out at the corner, Eden eventually relieving for Warrington with a spanking run and punt to Smith who had to send in touch to save. Warring. too now rallied and after an abortive drop by Isher- wood they swarmed round the home line Isherwoed made a determined effort to run through but was held by Thomas and Phillips near the lino. Warrington got possession several times, but the combination behind was poor. Harris would have scored once but for the slowness of the passing. Smith being enabled to run him in touch. Fish next tried a drop and was not far out, while Duckwertls ran cleverly on one occasion. Jones intercepted and should have scored but knocked on badly, a thieg which he was guilty of several times. The other backs were equally uncertain however, and chances continually went begging in a disheartening fashion. Ashurst respondedl to a punt by Thomas with a dashing run which resulted in him being hurt. Then Jones got possession, and beating his opponents should have scored easily but chose to pass to Harris, who hid no earthly chance with the ball thrown five yards behind him. Salford came again before the close and pretty passing by Phillips, Thomas and Crocker, ended in the latter doubling past Fish and giving Norris an easy journey to the line. Phillips failed at goal, and Salford were left winners of a hard fought game by 2 goals, 2 tries (10 points), to Warrington, 1 goal (2 points).

FATALITY AT DALLAIVI.

Recommendation by the Jury. Mr. Brigbouse,county coroner,conducted an inquiry at the Police Station last Thursday afternoon on trio body of Joseph Topping, who met with his death at Dallam Forge, at 10.15 on Tuesday forenoon. From the police report, it appears that the deceased, who was 63 years of age, was engaged with others in unloading steel axles from a railway truck by means of a steam crane. Be was in the truck, and fastened the clips round one of the axles, which was then hoisted, and as it hung in mid-air deceased overbalanced himself, falling on one end of the suspended axle. The clips then became un- fastened, and the axle fell upon the deceased's head, dividing his skull. Be was picked up and found to be dead. The axle was about 6ft lone and weighed 4cwt. Mr. Samuel Davies was foreman of the jury. Mr. Thomas Atherton, juu, represented Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, and Mr. Rogers, factory inspector, was also present. • The first witness called was John Topping, son of the deceased, who lived at 20, Houghton-street. He said his father was a forge labourer, and had been in the employ of the firm for about 39 years. Mr. Atherton explained that as the deceased was an old man lighter employment was found for him than that he had previously had to do. He had been engaged at this particular work about a week. Witness said he was also employed at the works, but ho did not snow how the accident happened. He had, however, seen hundreds of axles unloaded. There had been no previous accident eince the present kind of clip had been introduced. Joseph Wilkes, in the employ of the same firm, said he was engaged with the deceased on the morning in question, unloading the axles. Witness was driving the crane, and when the clip was put round the axle de- ceased gave the signal to witness to hoist up. The de- ceased made a stumble, and made a grab at the suspended axletas it was sailing round with the crane,in order to save hi:nself from falling. The result was that as soon as the deceased touched the axle it oscillated in mid-air, and the clips became unfastened. The axle fell upon the deceased's head, and when picked up be was dead. The clips were carefully examined by the jury, and suggestions made with a view to the plevention of a recurrence of a similar accident. Witness in answer to Mr. Rogers, said he had known the axle strike a waggon and not fall out. Mr. Downes (a juryman) expressed the opinion that the open space in the clip might have been the cause of the accident. The Coroner: Do you think so? I think the grip is just the same. Mr. Downes : If the grip had been a proper one would the accident have occurred ? The Coroner: No one anticipated a man getting hold of the axle. The firm provide for tie unloading of axles in:e certain way; they never anticipated that this unfor- tunate accident would have happened, and that a man would fall out of a waggon and get hold of one end of the axle. A Juryman : Why should the oscillation of the axle release it from the clip ? Mr. Atherton : We cannot account for that. The more fact that the clips have served their purpose for a number of years without any accident happening shows that they are suitable for the purpose. Mr. Dowries: These clips are much worn. Ido not think the accident would have happened if the clips had come closer together. The Coroner : Do you think the firm must provide apparatus on the off-chance of a man falling out of a waggon Mr. Downes : I don't say that. The Coroner (to Mr. Atherton) : Are the clips used for axlcs of different degrees in diameter ? Mr. Atherton : 'Desna axles are now all of One diameter ? & Juryman : Don't you think it would be safer if the Clips came closer together when holding the axles? Mr. Atherton : As a practical man I should say the grip of the clips was stronger when the clips are apart, and I think these are suitable clips for the purpose. Thy were made spec?, tly for this work. Thomas Gilmour as the next witness called, and stated that he had been 28 years in the employ of Messrs Pearson and Knowles, and 16 years of that time as fore- man. He thorouehly understood the work involved in unloading the steel axles as he had superintended the work. He had heard the opinion expreesed by jurymen that the axles would he been held securer if the clips were larger and fitted closer. He differed from that opinion. The axles were gripped better when the clips were apart. If they met togeth3r the axle would be more liable to slip out. When the deceased grasped the axle it caused it to " head up" in the air and the sudden jerk released it from the clips. Inspector Rogeis said he was certainly of opinion that te firm had exercised due cieflauce in carrying out the ht, but it was quite possible to have a little more Acety. It struck him that the clips did not come quite lose together as they might be made to do, but he not impute any carelessness to the firm. r. Dewnes said he must contend that the c lips.id mot Ogether sufficiently well to make them sate

Inspector Rogers said seeing that one of these axles bad been released from these clips, the firm might try by reducing the opening at the bottom, to make it less possible for a similar accident to occur. The Coroner stated that for the purpose for which they had been provided, the clips had been found useful and successful. It was never anticipated that a man would get hold of the axle. If the jury were going to say to the firm, you must make another set of clips, when were they going to prove that they were better than those in use ? They would not be able to find out whether they were better until another accident happened. He did not like to tell an employer how he Inuit carry on his business unless be was perfeetly justified in saying something. It might react on him if a suggestion of his were carried out, and resulted in an accident. The employer would not then be blameable, but the person who had made the suggestion. A verdict of" Accidental death" was returned, and the jury recommended that new clips be provided and that they should fit closer. The Coroner (to Mr. Atherton): No doubt that recommendation will receive your favourable considera tion ? Mr. Atherton : It will sir.

THE MAYORALTY OF WARRINGTON Conservative Criticism of Alderman

Roberts.

At a meeting of the Council of the Warrington Conservative Association on Thursday, Mr. PETER NAYLOR said that in regard to the selection of Alderman Roberts as Mayor, the bulk of the Conservative party had not been consulted in the matter, and he for one strongly objected to buch an action. Alderman Roberts was a man who, if he got the chance would murder every virtue of their Conservative principles. Mr. W. MILLS said he wished to enter a most emphatic protest against the selection for a Tory b!)rough of a Radical Mayor. He believed the dignity of the town could not be safely entrusted to the hands of Alderman Roberts who a few weeks ago used language in the Town Council which how- ever well it might be suited to Billingsgate was not usually employed in polite eociety. He also objected to Alderman Roberts's policy in regard to the licensing question. He sought to do away with licenses, which was unfair to the consumer and only tended to create a monopoly, Councillor BENNETT asked in the name of com- mon sense what else they could do but allow their opponents to nominate a candidate when they themselves could not provide a Mayor. Mr. PEACOCKE moved an amendment that they should not be bound to the compromise in muni- cipal elections for two years. This was seconded by Mr. H. HOUGHTON, but after a long discussion the amendment was with- drawn and the resolution was carried unanimously.

" ENVIRONMENT " AT THE FIELD CLUB. Sir,—On Friday, the 20th ult., Mr. A. J. Jolley in a paper on " Some) Effects of Environment." gave a very able reieumi of the arguments and facts of evolution as effected by the influence of the surroundings. Very naturally such a subject could not be dealt with fully without reference to the doctiine of final causes ; the subject is so intimately connected with teleology that it is almost impossible to deal with the one and leave the other untouched. The Lecturer showed that to the man who rejects revelation, Pope's dictum of " Looking up from nature to nature's God," is simply an impossibility and that Paley's Design Arguments had been Cone to death by Darwin, and in fact are now merely the wreck of a once goodly ship upon the sands of time. When the time for discussion arrived, then the mem- bers began to show the influence of their surroundings. One member used the argument of the time it requires to saturate wood with water; which, although a wooden argumeat, would be a good one if turned. It does re- quire a very long time to get evidential facts into a head previously steeped in traditional fables. Not a few of the speakers showed the influence of their guardian en- vironment by posing as evolutionists while retaining a special creationist creed. Not having the faintest con- ception of the necessities of logical consistency, they try to run with the hare of Paley and to hunt with the Dar- winian hounds, anti a terrible muddle they make of it ! I thought the President put the climax oa when he averred that, however puppies varied they were still puppies ! They never evoluted into cats ! Now our worthy chairman is one of a very numerous class, who must have the process of evolution perfenmed before their eyes like a conjuring trick. lock ! Presto ! This dog has become a pig!! Slightly childish, but very natural to minds steeped when young in special creationism and the limited chronology of tradition. My view of the matter is that no honour can accrete to God by maintaining obvious error ; that the God of truth must be best pleased to see His children seeking to attain the truth with all the earnestness and powers possessed by their finite nature, and, conversely, ever ready to abandon ideas impressed upon their young minds by too narrow an environment.— Yours, Scc., Penketh, October 23rd, 1899.

JOHN PEERS.

Sir,—ln reply to Mr. Councillor Monks' letter pub- Retied in your issue of the 14th iust., I find it necessary, after further inquiry, to admit the literal accuracy of Mr. Monks' statement with regard to Mr. Smith, and I theretore frankly withdraw my denial of it. 'Dais does not, however, in any material degree, affect the merits of the question, for it was never intended by the sub- committee of the nuance Committee from the first to permanently employ Mr. Smith. ludeEd, at the first of the meetings referred to by Mr. Monks, the sub-com- mittee absolutely declined to commit themselves to the permanent employment of any of my staff, and asked me to ascertain it they, or such of them as were not re- tained, would Wept a year's salary as compensation for loss of office. 1 found that they were not prepared to do tio, and at my second meeting with the sub-committee, further urged their retention. Mr. Coriumdor Green, to my surprise, at once offered to take them over on the seine conoitions upon which they held their appoint- ments with me, but when I asked that there should be an honourable understanding that they were subject to good behaviour, to be permanently retained, Mr. Green oeclined to give any such undertaking, the obvious in- ference being that the object was to get rid of some or one of them at suort notice. I pointed this out to the ' committee,and it was then euggested,l forget by whom, hut I believe by Mr. Mimics, that the committee might undertake to retain them for a shert period, and inci- dentally the question of Mr. Smith's position on the staff, it some such arrangement were made, was die- Graeae!. I insisted that Mr- Smith should not be sub- jected to the control of an officer of the Corporation. who, years before, had threatened him with what is now happening, but the committee declined to accede to this, and toe negetiatioes came to an end. I. of course, related the circumstances to Mr. Smith, and it appears that in a sabsequeut private coo- versation with Mr. Woodcock, Mr. Smith in- formed him that he approved of the course I had taken, and would abide by it. But, that Mr. Smith ever lead any opportunity given to him to enter the employ of the Corporation with any prospect ef per- manent retention, I utterly oeny. Worst does Mr. Monks say in his last letter? "The Sub-Committee declined to entertain or acknowledge any liability in respect of Mr. Smith and the other clerks in the office." And what dues Mr. Green say in his last letter? Refer- ring to myself, he says "he could make no possible mis- take as to the Committee's intention, for it was pointed out to him over and over again, that the Corporation would not require so large a staff, and, in particular, Mr. Smith was continually referred fa as the one least likely to be required." Yet, irethe face of this, Mr. Monks tells us that Mr. Smith nes himself to blame. The statements are not consistent. That this description does not apply to Mr. Green's veision, I think I shall presently be able clearly to demonstrate. Nor is it true that tney advised a enange. The Corperation applied to the Local Government Board for the powers in ques- tion in 1894, and Mr. Green, as chairman of his cemmit- , tee, had the conduct of the matter. This statement is a'so untrue, and without the shadow of any foundation in fact. Moreover, it i 3 absurd on the face of it, for the Act under which the provisional crders in question have been granted, specifically pro- vides that " the Local Government Board shall make sueli provisions as may seem necessary and just for the preservat;on of the existing interests at paid officers." It is obvious that there was no necessity for any attempt to influence the, Corporation. Mr. Green admire in his letter that at my second interview with the Sub-Com- mittee " the negotiations broke off." Yet, at a meeting of the Town Council, subsequent to that interview and held on the 7th January last, he said the negotiations were still proceeding. Evidently, in Mr. Green's view the expressions " the negotiations broke off " and "the negotiations are still proceeding " are synonymous, and Mr. Green, not satisfied with making such a series of reckless statements, must needs, in his unctuous recti- tude, lecture others upon their moral obligations. With regard to my interview with Mr. Fairhurst. Mr- Green states that my published version is very different from that iathe possession of the Committee, hut as he does not point out the al'egerl dieerepancy, I have to content myself with affirming that all I have stated is true both literally and in substance. Mr. Green is really talking nonsense. Neither the Local Government Beard nor the Treasury have anything to do with the matker,otherwise it would net have been necessary for the Corporition to promote special legislation. The report of the Local Government Board upon the Bill of the Corporation has been referred to and Mr. Green says it was distinctly in favour of the scheme of the Corporation. When the report was received an intimation was conveyed to me which origi- nated in official quarters, that it was " dead against me "and, as instructions had been given that upon no account was anyone to be allowed to see it en my behalf, my solicitor advised me te come to terms with the Com- mittee and I did so. Having since obtained a copy of the report I have been able to ascertain Mr. Green's notion of what is " distinctly in favour of the scheme of the Corporation." This scheme consisted ot five clauses, Nos. 111 ta 115. As to the clause 111 and 113 the Board express their opinion that they should not be allowed. As regards the prop )sed termination of my appointmerits the 13,.ard submit no proposals in either direction. They merely point out that "there is in udice a collector of poor tates, appointed by the Guardians under orders of the Poor Law Board, who is also assistant overseer under appJintment by tke Vestry " and they further pint out the proper means for terminating the appointments in question if the Committee of the House of Commons should be of opinion from the evidence that the circumstances of the case are such as to justify the adoption of that course. When the Bill was bsfore the House of Comm/ma:Com- mittte Mr. Boyce, standing Counsel to the Local G3a,ernment Board during the course of the proceedings addressed the following statement to the Counsel for the Corporation :—" You have power to appoint assistant- overseers, but you cannot get rid of the present person who is in office."

_ Mr. Green's assertion that " all recent legislation clearly suggested amalgamating such offices, and later still the Royal Commission on rating distinctly urged it in its report " is somewhat belatea. There is a later precedent, viz :—The legislation establishing Municipal Boroughs throughout the metropolis. In this case Mr Balfour declined to confer the duties with regard to the registration of voters upon officers who were not coin-. patent by experience and training to properly perform them, and the Town Clerks have been made responsible for the performance of those duties. I might considerably extend my criticism of Mr Green's asseverations, but the process, whilst necessary owing to Mr. Green's challenge, is decidedly unpalat- able and lam glad to be able to turn to the merits of the question, which was in the first instance the only one in dispute —the treatment of the staff. Both Mr. Monks and Mr. Green admit that they ought to be corn- , pensated, but they think this should be done at my ex- pease. Why they can take such a view I cannot imagine. My retirement has been forced upon me by the Corporation at a loss of one third of my net income from the offices in question and because in the process the retirement of my clerks has been forced upon them also by the Corporation it is suggested that I should compensate them. The compensation paid to me was ch.arly not intended to include any con pensation:to my staff, because a separate compensation to them was sug- gested by the sutecemmittee at my fint interview with them. The staff were paid out of my own pocket and had I chosen p2rsonally to devote more tinsta to the duties et the office instead of employ- ing others, I should have been entitled to commensurate compensation. Oa the other band, had my clerks held official positions, they would have been receiving the same remuneration, and my emoluments would not have been reduced, and in that case, they would have been legally entitled to compensation, so that the whole case turns upon the legal quibble as to official appoint- ment. The duties of the staff have been performed in a most efficient manner, and so far as a comparison is 'possible, (and this is so in the case of the collection of rates) they have been more efficiently performed thanthe similar duties devolving upon officers of the Corporation and at a smaller cos:. I take Mr Green's own figures, for in a letter published by him on the thh March last, he states the cost of collection by the Corporation at 1•07 per cent. I take it, that the calculation was based upon the work of the immediately preceding year. In the same year, the collection in the Overseers office was £29,001 21 9J., and the cost, readily and absolutely ascertainable, because it arises in respeet of kseparate appointment, with separate pay,was £285, equal to •983 per cent. There was no occasion te dis- turb my staff, because there is plenty of work for them to do. If the staff of the Borough Treasurer's depart- moat is able to undertake the duties, the obvious infer- ence is that they have not enough to do now, so that , there will be a paper saving by reason of the fuller em- ployment of a staff beyond present requirements. Be- yond this, there will, no doubt, be some saving to begin with by the employment of less experienced and cheaper labour. That there will be any real saving in the long run, Ido not, for one moment believe. The change will not be to the public advantage, and an injustice has been done in the saerlice of one staff for the benefit of another.—l am, sir, your obedient servant, J. L. TUNSTALL.

Warrington, October 20th, 1899. THE RATEPAYERS' ASSOCIATION AND MR. J. SHAW-GREF,N.

Dear Sir,—The announcement made het week that Messrs. A. J. Jolley and L. Greening were being nomina- ted, and would probably b 3 elected to fill certain vacant seats in the Council Chamber, would I believe be hailed with satisfaction in most quarters of the town. The first-named gentleman has not only rendered con- spicuous se:vices, but has shown such exceptional administrative ability as a member of the Board of Guardians as to mike it desirous that he should have an opportunity of rendering services on behait of his native town.

My special object, howevsr, in writing is to express a note of regret that it should be the intention of the Ratepayers' Association to bring a candidate into the field against the re-election of Mr. J. Shaw Green. That a Ratepayers' Association well managed and judicially handled can render valuable services no one I think will venture to deny. Such associations have and are rendering efficient services in various pars of the Coun- try and events have proved the wisdom of having a siinilar association in Warrington- The attempt however on the part of this association to oust a man of the calibre of Mr. Green is to my mind to perpetrate a " blunder" and is caleulated not to bring any credit to the association. And their best friends will not hesitate to tell them so.

It is scarcely nee issary for me to say that Mr. Green has a greater knowledge of the Corporation's finances than any other member of the Council. He has not only merited the position he occupies as ohairman of the Finance Committee bat his business experience as an accountant naturally adapts him for the position. His colleagues in electing him to that position certainly showed their collective wisdom. His is not a case at all of a " round peg being placed in a square hole." In addition to his financial knowledge Mr. Green is a man of more than average ability and he is, which is the greatest force of all—a man of moral fibre—and thus possesses the qualities which help to meke an ideal councillor. He has well-earned and I believe does com- mand the respect and confidence of most of the inhabit- ants of Warrington and it is generally acknowledged that he i 5 one of the ablest councillors that the town possesses. I have no hesitation in saying that if the present Council were to be asked to select six men to represent them on an important matter, Mr. Green would be one of the six to be so chosen, and not to b 3 the last of the six to be chosen either.

It seems to me very singular that as he was the nominee at one time of the Association that they should at this important time turn their backs upan him-- especially after the arduous and efficient serviccs he has rendered the town. Their action, en the face of it, savour' either of Ingratitucle or thoughtlessness. By making the attempt they will only alienate the sympathy of many citizens from themselves and thus prevent the carrying out of many good objects they may have in view. By carrying out a policy of this kind they will simply be copying the worst side of party politics in municipal life which seaks to " oust" excellent men, because they happen to be in the camp of their political opponents irrespective of the merit of such individuals. One of the objects of a Ratepayers' Association is to check this evil so unfortunately prevalent, and thfrefore, the Warring- ton Ratepayer' Association,lunless they can show good cause to the contrary, are, in making the attempt to prevent the return of Mr. Green, only stultifying them- selves. _ _ _

In coiclusion I will say that if he was qualified when they nominated him, some years ago, he is aftar the ex- perience gained,doubly qualified now.—Yours faithfully, E. ENGLAND.

LYMM.

A Farmer's Son's Death at a Football Match.

lAt the Sessions House, Lymm, on Monday, before Mr. Dobson, coroner, an inquest was held touching the death of Samuel Edgar Williamson, of Warburton. James Williamson, father of the deceased boy, said he was a farmer. The boy asked leave to go to the foot- ball match at Lymm. He was in good health, and a little after two he let him go. He also got his mother's leave to go. He did not see him alive again. He was a healthy boy and not subject to fits. Peter Brooks, labourer, said he was at tbe match on Saturday, and about four o'clock he saw the boy stand- ing near him. He dropped against him and said," I am going to die." He did net hear him speak again though he was near him for ten minutes. Soon after Sergeant Garner came, he " stretched out and gave two bits of twiches and was gone." He shot straight out. He was a straneer to all around, Sergeant T. Garner said he saw deceased, and he was among the spectators and lying down. His face was flushed and his eyes fixed. His pulse was still. He re- sorted to artificial respiration for some time, but he was quite satisfied the lad was dead. He had him conveyed to the Fleece Hotel, where he was soon after attended by Dr. Williamsm. Dr. W. W. Williamson said he examined the body of the deceased. He had known the family over ten years and incidentally the boy. The family were of a highly nervous disposition, and the boy hurried away from his work to get to the match. He believed that he died from angina pectoris, which g,enerally 61ed to sudden death. He thought that no time had been lost, and Sergeant Garner in trying to restore respiration had done everything possible for the boy. A verdict according to the medical evidence was re- turned.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, 00T013ER 27. 1899.

Lltloll CONSIDERABLY SURPRISED,

(BY THE "SCORCHER.")

Some people—certainly not natives of Runcorn— cousider lam " drawing the long-bow," when I credit Runcorn with being the best Rugby combin- ation this season has producei. Yet, if they follow the doings of the team as closely as myself; watch each individual that goes to mike the whole, and weigh all up, comparatively, with other inlividual players and teams, they would see wherein the great secret of Runcorn's success lies. Again, let us go back to the date—March 4th last when " the rut in the lute," consequent on the attitude of certain Runcorn players, was repaired ; and to when Ritncorn faced Oldham as a united whole, at Runcorn, and beat them. Why! Siuce that date—with the single exception of the etock- port match—Runcorn have won 12 Northern league contests out of 13, and eleven of the victories were consecutive. These figures are authentic, and there is no " long-bow" about them. It is, however, more particularly with the Leigh match that I have to deal this week. The result was a surprise to Leigh. and to some extent a surprise to Runcorn. The home team were naturally confident of victory, but ow- ing to the sligkt disorganization in their ranks some doubt was felt as to the issue. As I observed last week, however, the doubt was but sufficient to add a bit of spice to the encounter. A peculiarity of the game was the way in which Leigh always secured possession from tie scrummages, a feat they accomplished almost evey time: perhaps Runcorn did get it half-a-dozen times : but though Leigh had the making of of the game every time, the speed and keenness of the home forwards inevitably triumphed, and what should have been an advantage often proved other- wise. At back Leigh were far outclassed, and not on a single occasion did the home team allow them to get anywnere near their uprights to hazard a drop, though Runcorn backs often ventured a shot, and one from Butterworth proved productive. Of course it is natural to suppose that Leigh attributed their loss to the fact that Dunbavin was absent, but no one will say that they were any worse handicapped than were the" Linnets" who were without the services of Faulkner. Unless of course the secret lies in the fact that Nickson was a better man than Hall; a concession that Leigh would hesitate to grant. The game was a hard one throughout, and the victory gained one of Run- corn's most brilliant successes, which clearly illus- trated that Runcorn have a first reserve of a very high order, and one well capable of uphold- ing the prestige of the club. I noticed that Jones the fast wing man was absent and some people expressed the opinion that he should be reinstated, to those people 1 would say I "Be patient ?" The people who select the team are desirous of winning every match, and are saving and preserving their men, as a result of the lesson learnt at Stockport. One thing that the match will bring about is certain, and that is a spirited en- counter at Leigh. In the meantime there will have to be an improvement on Leigh's part or a deterior- ation on the part of Runcorn if things are to be re- versed.

Next Saturday the 03unty match takes preced- ence, and eight of the Runcorn fifteen are again called upon to battle for the"three wheat sheaves." They are: Houghton, back; J Butterworth, three- quarter; Jolley and Faulkner, halves; Lightfoot, Walker, Gayter, and Farmer, forwards. They can all ba depended upon to play for all they are worth and so far express willingness to turn out.
