The Council is in full agreement with your Committee on this point, and would remind you that this was the original prouosition with regard to the nurse. Your statement that " I myseld proeosed to raise money to employ a nurse as part of the p trochial staff " dues not Wye a correct actiount of the origin of the Nerse Fund. It is within the recollection of some members of this Council that the late Mrs. Henry White always asserted that she origina- ted the idea of a District Nur te, and viewed with alarm your subsequent attempt to make parochial capital out of it. She worked hard to effect the compromise you refer to, and it was the unsatisfactory result of her endeavours in this direction thet gave rise to such misgiving on the part of the Nonconformists of the town. In view of these circumstances, your claim to be the founder of the Distrfct Nurse Fund is open to question. Whilst unanimous in their condemnation of your unfairness ha the matter, Nonconformists were divided as to the policy of dealing with the situation. One section asserted that you had changed the letter of the scheme, toe more readily to obtain public money, whilst the spirit of your policy remained as sectarian as before ; they therefore refused te subscribe at all, The other section decided to keep in touch with the matter, hoping that eventually some more equitable arrangement might be effected. We are simply stating the facts of the case at the time the Nurse Fund was started. The Free Church Council voices the wrung which individuals could only press at the risk of being embroiled in a purely persoaal conflict. The Council gladly reeagnisee that you do not claim the right to the chair MI the Vicar of the Parish, bet we would point out that this is the rule which you adopted. viz : —" The Vicar of Runcorn shall be e •-officio Chairman," and in view of your admission that the nurse is in no sense a parochial but a dis- teict nurse, the wisdom of your accepting the chairmanship as Vicar if Runcorn, which you say the kindness of the promo- ters promoted them to offer you, is open to question, but that your successor should be able to claim the chair after you in virtue of the rule relating to the chairmanship is sin unques- tionable wrong, and we are surorised that your Committee should fail to recognise the unfairness which is involved. As to the constitution of the Committee and the association of the work with the Vicarage, we think that in common fair- ness, the chairman of the committee should never have allowed the influence of the Established Church to preponder- ate so larzely in what is a purely District Nurse Institution. It is indeed odious to have to deal with an unfair situation, but if your committee can imagine the Wesleyan Methodist Minister arranging for himself and successor a perpetual chairmanship, accepting a committee with 90 per cent. of his own communion upon it, and identifying his manse with the transaction of all the business Ira the case of a distinctly distlict organisation they will realise the feelings of those wile view with censure any attempt to turn public charity to sectarian ends. Your attempt to apportion the contributions is most unfair. We would remind you that all the money has been obtained for a district nurse on a purely district totals, and that many of the subscribers are oblivious of the facts about which :we complain. But if a scheme of distribution is to be attempted, then the substantial sums given by the various public companies end clubs must stand to the credit or a town's nurse controlled by a popular authority without sectarian bias,for these lIIIMB would doubt- less be transfeezed to such an organisation if its establish- ment became a necessity, such necessity we should deeply esaplere and would do all in our power to avert. The Coun- cil trusts that your Cenanaittes en reconsidering these points, will take seine steps to redress the wrongs complained of. As to your personal Chairmanship, we think you would hold it with a firmer grip if it were left in the hands of the annual meeting of the Nurse Fund. As tothe constitution of the Committee, we think the adoption of a number of additional names woald meet the situation. As to the place of meeting, we feel that the district character of the Nurse Fund shou d not be obscured by reasons of personal convenience on the part of the members of the Committee, but that the place or places of meeting should be in harmony with the unsectarian ghat acter of the nurse's work. The Council does not at the present moment challenge the practical good which you say ehe Nurses have done, nor does the Council in any sense seek to imperil the success of the enterprise. It is a work we have et heart equally with your Committee, and we repeat our assurance ot increased support if this institution is put on a setiefectory basis. The present one-sided constitution of the fond is theigrevest peril to its success, and a stumbl- ing block to those who would like to help in the work, and we teal that it will become necessary to appeal te the piebiie of Runcorn in Lhe interests of a separate Nurse Fund unless these wr eases of which we complain are redressed.-4 am sir, yours faitillully,

TOWN.

An abortive property sale to)k place at Newton Market Inn, Earlestown, on Tuesday evening. There were two lots offered. The first consisted of five leasehold dwelling-houses, Nos. 266 to 274 inclusive, St. Helens New-road, Newton-Common, together with a small plot of freehold land adjoin- ing thereto, and containing thirty-nine square yards or thereabouts. The bidding ran up to £425, at which price the property was withdrawn. The second lot consisted of four dwelling-houses situate on the North side of Crow-lane (corner of Clarence- street) intended to be Nos. 180, 182, 184 and 186 Crow-lane West. Tne bidding in this case rose to .£5OO, but as there was no advance the property was withdrawn.

ST. HELENS.

SAD STORY OF ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.

About half-past seven on Saturday morning, Solomon Williams, an elderly man, who lodged at 115, Harris. street, St. Helens, made a determined effort to commit suicide by cutting his throat with a breadknife. It appears that Williams only left the Providence Hospital on Friday morning, after being there a considerable time suffering from an old internal complaint. During the day he went to Liverpool and consulted a physician as to his condition, and the opinion of the medical man being a most unfavourabie one,this seems to have preyed on his mind, and caused him to attempt suicide. It is stated that he was in the Providence Hospital about seven years ago, having attempted to commit suicide on that occasion. He is in a very critical state. Sr. HELENS MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

The nomination of candidates for the approaching municipal elections at St. Helens took place on Tuesday. in accordance with general anticipation there will be contests in three cut ot the nine wards into which the borough is divided, namely, in North Eccleston Ward, where the retiring councillor.Mr B B Glover (C),is being opposed by Mr Thomas Stevenson (L) ;in Hardshaw Ward, where Councillor Peter Grundy (14, is being opposed by Mr C D Fothergill ; in Parr Ward, where the return of Councillor Ryder (C) is being opposed by Dr O'Keefe (L). The unopposed returns are those of Coun- cillor J B Leath (L) for Central Ward, Councillor A J Foote (L U) for North Windle, Councillor Walter L Shaw (C) for South Eccleston, Councillor Dr Bates (C) for East Sutton, Councillor Edward Johnson (C) for West Sutton, and Councillor T M'Lachlan (L) for South Windle.

DEATH OF LADY GAMBLE.

The many friends of Sir David Gamble, Bart., C. 8., will receive with regret the announcement of the death of Lady Gamble, which took place at Windlehurst, St. Helens, at 6.30 on Monday night. Lady Gamble WAS seventy-three years of age,and for the past eight months she had been confined to bed suffering from an inter- nal complaint and heart trouble. For some time past it was evident that she was rapidly grawing worse. She was attended by Dr. Masson, of St. Helens, who has at various times had consultations with Sir W. H. Broadbent, of London, Dr. Dreschfeldt, of Manchester, Dr. Carter, and Dr. Twyford, of St. Helens. Since Saturday evening Lady Gamble had been unconscious, and passed quietly away in the presence of most of the members of her family, towards whom at this time there is universal and pro- found sympathy. Lady Gamble was a native of St. Helens, and since her marriage with Sir David resided continuously in the town. She was the daughter of the late Mr. Thomas Haddock, of Ravenhead, St. Helens, who was a leading member of the firms of Clare and Haddock, of the S. Helens Colliery, and Bromilow Had- dock, aad Ca.. of Ravenhead. She was married to Sir David at the St. Helens Parish Church on January 26, 1847, and her family consisted of eleven children, all of whom survive, as well as twenty-five grandchildren. Throughout her life Lady Gamble devoted herself to her family and the poor of the neighbourhood. The latter found in her a most kindly friend, whose acts of generosity, carried out in the quietest possible man- ner, were a source of comfort and help to many.

Oa tile contrary they strongly protest against" it, and find in it a hindrsncs to their in lividual support of the fund. To',the Chairman of the Committee if the Run torn Dis:ritt Nursing Mused. Dear sir, —I am instructed by the Executive Committee of the Runoorn Evangelical Free Church Council to represent to the Cornmittes of the District Nu-se Fund that there is a strong conviction ia the minds of Noneonformists that the Mares Fund, which they largely support, is so much con- trolled ty adherents of the Established Chea—h, that it prastieally represents the Christian philanthropy of that Church only. The Council very much reerets that there should be any cause whatever for this state of feeling with regard to the Nurse Fund, especially because t hey are in full and hearty sympathy Iwith such noble and sacred work as ministering to tbe 'Sick ones among the peor of the town. But that such cause exists, and has existed for a considerable titre, is undeniable. The Cam cntttee of the Nurse Fund are doubtless aware that when the Nurse Fund was started. many Nonconformists refused to support it because they oojyte 1 to tli Irate t hat the Vicar of Runcorn was to be e..: officio Chairman. They believed that such a rule was an unjust assumption that an official of the Established Church had an inherent right to be at the h-ad of a movement which represented the Christian charity of 'other churches besides that oc the Established Church. M nv Nonconfermisto at the present time share the same objection. Another reason that makes it impos- si.le for Noneonformists to give undivided support to the Nurse Fund is found in the fact that the preseet committee of the present Nurse Fund, whilst consisting of twelve members includes ten adherents of the Established Church. The coatrol, therefore, is very one-sided, consider- ing that Nonconferrnists subscribe so much towards the fund. They feel that they are not adequately or justly represented on the Board of Management. Further, almost all the offilial meetings in connection with the Nurse Fund are held at the Vicarage In the opinion of the Council this arrsngemeat emphasises the conviction that the Nnrse Fund is rapidly becoming a permanent institution of the Estsblished Church, instead of duly seoresenting the philanthropic work of mernhers of other Churches as well The Council is deeply anxious to press upon the committee of the Nurse Fund the seriousness of this protest. It springs out of the conviction that a movement representine the Christian charity of many Churches should not be coutrollect or monopolises' by any one of them, as thorgh arty particular Church had an exclusive right te distribute the offerines of the members if other churches. The Council would rejoice if every hindrance to the harmonioes working of the Nurse F. el were removed, so that Noecoe- formists might w:_ts increased Rupee -t and con- fidence, maintain this saced ministry amongst the p sor cad in this spirit, the Council weals, in the sole inte-ests of this work, to the Committee of the Nurse Fund to apply the remedy to the existing dissatisfection. Child not the Com- mittee pledge themselves to do all in their power to secure three simple. yet. effective alterations in the constitution of the Nurse Fund ? (1). To make it a rule that the Chairman of the Committee, instead of holding office et-officio as at pre- sent, shall be elected annually. (2). To enlarge the Corn- rnitteelso as to give adequate repre entation to Noeconformiat subscibers. (3). To provide that all official meeLings in con- neetion with the Nurse Fund, shall be held, ei her on neutral ground, or suL:essively in so.ne building belonging to one of the churches of the town. The Fighting at Rietfontein. Over One Hundred British Killed and Wounded.

Tee Prose Association says the War Office had received no further details up to six o'clock on Wednesday even • in of General Sir George Waite's engagement at Rite: fontein, near Ladysmith, on Tnesday, and the nature of tie battle is not yet known, although the list of !Tilled and wounded on the British sid3 /eaves no doubt that it was severely contested and the victory again dearly bought. The strength of Sir George White's force is noc precisely known, but it is believed to have consisted of three infantry battalions, five batteries of artillery,three cavalry regiments, and 500 Natal Volunteers. It is de- finitely knows that the Ist Battalion Gloueeatershite Re- glement, Ist Devonshire Regiment, 19t Hussars, sth Lancers, 42nd Field Battery, 53rd Feld Battery, No. 10 Mountain Battery, and the Natal Carabiacers (Volun- teers) were units, as they suffered loss in the engage- ment. Our total losses ware over one hundred in killed and wounded.

The enemy is believed to have suffered considerably. The Boers own officially the loss of 100 killed, and we have on our hands over 300 prisoners wounded,amongst whom are se v era I men holding prominent official p3sl- - ions in the Transvaal.

The Fighting at Glencoe. Guns not Captured. (Central News Telegram. Delayed in trammission). Glencoe Camp, October 23rd, 1.50 p.m. We have bad an anxious time after our great victory on Friday. We imagined we were clear of the enemy for some days at any rate, but we were entirely mistaken. Wen the troops came hack from the field it was Lund, with mueh regret, that the reported capture of the whole of their guns was a incorrect. The enemy succeeded in taking them off before our gallant fellows stormed the hill.

_ British Prisoners at Pretoria. The Fate of the Hussar Squadron. PRETORIA, OCT. 22.

- - The prisoners who were taken on Friday near Dundee were entrained at DAnnhaueser. T,,ey filled ten covered trucks. The officers travelled in first-class car- riage, and a separate van was provided for two of their number, who were wounded. The men affect indifference aud spend their time chiefly in smoking. They sleep in the grand stand. The names of the officera are :—Lieutenant-Colonei Maj °apt lin Pollok, Captain Lens- dale, and Lieutenants De Mesurier, Jarvie, (irimshaw, Maiendie, and Shore. SUDDEN DEATIi AT WINWICK,

The sudden death of a man named Thomas Britch, of Wairington-road, Winwick, 75 years of age has been reported to the Newton police. The deceased was formerly a tabourer on the St.Elelens and Sankey Canal in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Company,but had not done any work for tho past 5 or 6 years, during which time he has been in receipt of a pension from the Company. At the time he ceased work he injured himself by overliftinv, causing a rupture. About noon on Tuesday he left home in apparently good health. saying he was going to watch the potato getters. Whilst in the field he began to be unwell, and was taken home in a float and died about an hour afterwards in the presence of his wife and others, not having been seen by a doctor. The facts were reported to the coroner.

ODDFELLOWS' ANNIVERSARY AT EARLESTO WN.

The anniversary dinner of the Loyal Viaduct lodge Ne. 2686 of the Independent Order ot Oddfellows, MU., was held at the Wellington Hotel, Earlestown, on Satur- day evening. There were 62 present to dinner, which wa- served in first class style. After the cloth had been removel the proceedings were presided over by P.G. Themes F. Roberts,the vice-chairman being N.G.Joseph T Banton, and amongst the visitors were Bros. P.P.G.M. John Tunstall (Liverpool), D.G.M. Salt (Warrington), and P.G M. J R Barnwell of Warrington. The Chairman said that,as thee perhaps were aware, was the 57th anniversary of the lodge and he offered a sincere welcome to all prescnt, and trusted that the words spek n that night might prove of lasting good to all, and tend to further strengthen the principles of Odd- fellowehip. In that room they bad present with them several gentlemen who would doubtless speak to them of the working and benefits of the greet Order with which they were connected. One was an old friend, who attended the meetings for many years. The other gentlemen although not so well known to them as a ledge, were no straneers te Oddfellowship, but were earnest workers in thet noble community. the Manchester Unity, and in any ceese which bad for its object the advancement of their fellow men, and to them all on behalf of the Viaduct Lodge, he offered a special welcome. He trusted that nothing would arise to disturb the harmony of their meeting, but that all would enjoy that night's entertain- ment and go away, net empty handed, but with a smell supply of u,eful knowledge and a determination to keep more in touch with the lodge to wlhcla they helinged and prove themselves worthy the name of Oddfellows. (Applause.) He concluded by giving the toast of "Th. Queen," which was drunk with musical honours.

P.P.G.M. Thomas Tunstall in giving the toast of "The Manchester Unity "said it was the greatest society on earth amongst Friendly Societies. Its root was in Man- chester but its branches went to every part of the world. (Hear, hear.) But in all its working, he thought there was plenty of room for reform. He was glad to see they had gone in for the old age pension, and wore supporting it strongly. He thought it would be a great boon to every member who reached the age of 85, and he did not think they, as a lodge, could do nctter than support the old age pension scheme. (Hear, heir.) Bro. Salt of Warrington responding to the toast said the M.U., as it was at present was certainly the gran- dest organisation so far as Friendly Societies were con- cerned, of any in the world. During the past year the M.U. had expended practically in round figures £2,500 per day in sick pay, counting each day in the year, and they should consider for a moment what distress,poverty and pain that amount had alleviated. Beyond thst it had buried 370 members per working day, and had found the money to provide decent interment for those wno had passed away and a little to assist afterwards, those who had been left. That only spoke of what they might call the absolutely essential part of the werk of the M.U. No doubt it was the most beneficial portion of the benefits that accrued to the membership of the Order, but they had to look not only to to-lay, but to the benefits the members must received in the future, and to make provision for that he was pleased to say during the past year they had put away £1,200 per working day. It was the duty of every member of the M.O .to strive towards perfection and if they could n,t do it, they could at least deserve it, and in his opinion one of the first steps the M U. will have to take into consideration was the condition of those known as the ins lvent lodges of the Order. He knew that if a business man could not pay 203. in the pound he was called a bankrupt, but if a lodge was not solvent it was either through carelessness or mis- management. He believed they should not do justice to the older lodges of the Order, and to those members of the Order who had borne the brunt of the work in times past until they centralised tbeir sick and funeral funds to make it impossible for any man to go to tbe Unity and not receive the benefits which had been pro- mised to him. The heads of the Unity could see no necessity for helping the insolvent lodges beyond a grant, or taking them over on the Unity funds. They would not get justice as things were arranged at pre- sent. He wanted the members individually to take an active interest in the Order to which they belonged, to send men to the A.M.C. prepared to kick up a row when they saw things were not going right, and there were many things on the A.M.C. whicti were utter rot. If he had the honour to be sent as a delegate, and was sup- ported as he was the last time,be could assure them that ' that rottenness should be utterly exposed. (Hear,hear.) They had, on the Board of Directors gentlemen who were worthy of every respect, who had worked bard for the position they occupied, but they had man who had been there far too long. He could see avenues opening b"fore them that might lead them in the not very dis- tant future to much grander results than had been at- tained yet. If honesty, integrity and intelligent interest was taken in the M U. they might go on and make the Order felt in every home in the coontry. He hoped that the Mx. would be first in their minds so far as helping those who needed help was concerned. (Applause.) Bro. P.P.G.M. William Gilmour, in proposing the toast of " The Grand Master and Board of Directors " coupled with it the name of P.P.G.M. John Tunstall, of Liverpool. Bro. Tunstall, in response, said he looked forward to attending that anniversary each year with pleasure. He thouelit that was h;s 28th or 29:k appearance at the anniversary, and he hoped, although he could scarcely expect it, to attend as many more. There was no insti- tution in this country could boast such a membership or capital as that Order had. (Hear, hear). In connec- tion with insolvent lodges, as they were termod, he thought they had heard him say that in the future they would have more difficulty its de tling with surplus than with ins-lvent lodges. He thought at the last Board meeting but one there were no less than 158 applications for appropriating surplus capital. If there was a weak lodge in a district or there was a weak district, in place of allocating the surplus of the Lodge or district amongst themselves, it would be a glorious work to and over that surplus to remove the liabilities of the poorer lodges. There was not a lodge in the Liverpool district in a deficiency, and what could be done there, could be done elsewhere. The question of old age pensions had been alluded to. He had been a strong advocate of State aided pensions. He bad stated it was a disgrace to this country, that at the close of the 19th Century, at the close of the glorious Victorian era, that the very men who had been the means of build- ing up this great empire were left at the present time to the tender mercies of the poor law. He trusted the Board would see their way to effect many reforms in connection with their unity, reforms which would tend to help on the good work of Friendly Societies in this country, reforms which would materially benefit every- one of them and those who were depending upon them, and that it would be a pattern for every other society in this country to look up te. (Applause.) Bro. J R Barmwell of Warrington, gave toast " The Viaduct 'Ledge," and Bra Thane (secretary) responded referring briefly to the principal items on the balance sheet. The income on behalf of the Sick and Funeral fund was £673 93 61,1, made up chiefly of contributions £431 IT-3 711 and interest for invested capital £133 18e le' the latter item equalling a contribution of nearly 6; 81 per member. The expenditure was £4BB 1.83 ad out!of which they paid £413 5i in Sick Pay and Funeral donations, as they had just beard, the Unity payed about £2500 per day in sic' s pay, but their lodge paid about £1 per day. The present membership of the Lodge was 412, and the capital £2,742 7s sd, and al- though the pre3eat year had been exceedingly heavy both in Sick Pay and District Funeral levies, he trusted they would at least have no loss on the year's working. (Hear, hear.) The Gther toasts were "The Ashton-in-Makerfield die- trict" proposed by P.P.G.M. Wilson and responded to by P.G. George Barra.tt. MEETING OF HOUNDS AT DARESBURY,

Monday's venue of the North Cheshire Hunt was Dareibury Hall, on the Warrington side. Good woodland work was done in Rose Wood covers, and several foxes were rattled about, hounds tasting blood. Daresbury Willows produced a stout-hearted fox, and getting away on a good scent, hounds bad a nice quarter of an hour's bat right-handed on to Stretton,wh ere the pack killed in the open. Grinis- ditch covers yielded, but the fox was chopped be- fore he could get away. The Whitley gorse was too thick to allow of the quarry heading out, and here a nice day's sport concluded.

SODIFTRIN4 ABOUT TRH STORY.

On his way to visit his cousin, Sir Harry Beauwerth, Alec Beauworth, heir to thc title, is set upon by two men and knocked senseless. Sir Harry Beauworth has a bad reputation. Bob Rabbitts, son of the evil-looking landlord of the' Dog and Duck," revives Alec, and de- termines, as ha has the chance, to enter Sir Harry's servioes for a time to see that Alec comes to no harm. Sir Harry has one daughter, Ruby, a beautiful and un- sophisticated girl, who has never been adewed to see the world, and whom her father intends to marry to an Australian. James Felton, who with his father, To Felton; appears to have a powerful interest over his. These Feltons arc ne men who tried to kill Alm, though he never saw their faces, and it is obvious that they acted at the instigation of the baronet, who had some secret reason for wishing bo be rid of the heir. Also when he sees Ruby, falls in love with her, and not knowing his danger determines, if possible, to stay and win her.

Though he has no intention of allowing Ruby to marry Alec, Sic Harry Beauwortla, plays upon Alec's f In order to induce him to make a will in RubT's favour. Alec consents, and hereafter his destruction is practically decided upon by Sir Harry and the Felt,n's, so as to pave the way for a division of all the money and the estates which in the ordinary course, would have fallen to Alm The baronet is in reality personating the real baronet who died many years ago in the Bush and the Feltons, knowing this secret, blacktuLa Sir Harry Beauworth and make him an ally in their nefarious schemes. With the assistance of a movable floor in his bedroodi Alec is preS- pitated into a dungeon and is left there t 3 starve. His thrilling attempts to escape and the subsequent adven- tures which befall him and the heroine Ruby impart a Monte Cristo fl svour to this romance which draws to a spirited and satisfactory conclusion.

THE BOERS. A School-Girl's Amusing Essay. At a class in one of our Warrington schools the scholars had recently to write an essay on " The Boers," and some of the productions were distinctly funny. We append the essay of one of the girls in the class, which will be difficult to beat for unconscious humour and originality. It is as follows : " THE BOERS." The early settlers of Cape Colony were the Boers. In 1896 a war was begun between the French and the English. At first the Boers ware looking very dirty and were very lazy. They are getting a little better by their colour and are doing more work. In 1886 Cape Colony was turned to a gold mine. The French were building buts, farms, aud gold mines. The French were plough- ing the ground and the English came and took the cpuntry off them. When the English were going to war the French went into the Orange Free State, and others into Natal. The English when they were going to war asked the French if they would lead them the Boers till they had finished the war, and they would have them when they had finished war. The English did not mean to give them back to the French."
